Executive Thesis & Before You Read

MPS Limited turns manuscripts, textbooks and corporate training into finished digital products for the world's largest publishers. Its Research segment earns a 48% EBITDA margin, ten-year operating cash flow roughly equals profit, and the company carries no bank debt. Against that, the largest customer is 17.3% of revenue, goodwill and intangibles are 52.5% of assets, and generative AI is deflating the price of content production. At the ₹2,297.00 close of 12 November 2026 the expected 12-month return is between -1.9% and +6.2%, depending on the earnings basis. The verdict is WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT, with a ₹2,070 to ₹2,150 watch zone (only ₹2,070 clears 1.5:1 on both bases), a ₹2,005 invalidation level and 0% allocation today.

Chapter 0

What the company actually does. MPS Limited (NSE: MPSLTD; BSE 532440) turns research manuscripts, school and nursing textbooks and corporate training into finished digital products. Its customers are the world's largest publishers, universities, health systems and corporations [1]. It reports three segments. Research Solutions is 60% of FY26 revenue and about two-thirds of segment profit. It runs peer review, production and research-integrity work for scholarly publishers on platforms such as DigiCore and HighWire. Education Solutions (27%) builds adaptive courseware. Since February 2026 it also owns Unbound Medicine's subscription clinical-reference apps. Corporate Learning (13%) builds custom e-learning and simulations through Tata Interactive, TOPSIM and Liberate [1], [2]. FY26 consolidated revenue was ₹768.37 Cr, EBITDA ₹235.85 Cr and audited basic EPS ₹102.11. Some 99.5% of revenue is earned in foreign currency [1]. In H1 FY27 revenue grew 17.8% to ₹448.5 Cr and EBITDA grew 39% to ₹154 Cr, a 34.3% margin. Management raised the full-year EBITDA floor from ₹300 Cr to at least ₹310 Cr [2].

The business earns a COMPOUNDER_PASS, but the desk does not buy at ₹2,297.00. The probability-weighted 12-month value is ₹2,440 (+6.2%) on current earnings and ₹2,254 (-1.9%) on year-end earnings, and reward-to-risk to the ₹2,005 invalidation level is 0.39:1 or worse. The tape is at the Stage 3/Stage 4 boundary. On the more generous earnings basis the support zone offers 1.79:1 at ₹2,150 and 5.23:1 at ₹2,070. On year-end earnings it offers 0.63:1 and 2.63:1, so only the ₹2,070 end clears the 1.5:1 hurdle on both. MPSLTD is not held (0 shares), so the only open decision is whether to earmark cash.

Why the price is where it is, and our judgment. The stock has run a full cycle inside twelve months. It traded at about 42 times trailing profit in March 2025, 16 times in March 2026, 29 times in August 2026 and 22.7 times now. Over that span earnings per share grew 16% in FY26 and the Q2 FY27 print beat [2]. Datamatics, NIIT Learning Systems and Newgen are all in Stage 4 and have fallen 18% to 56% in a year as the market priced AI disruption into content and services names. MPS has held up better than that group but has started to roll over. We think the business deserves a premium to those peers. It earns a 38.7% ROCE against 20% to 25% for them, converts profit to cash, and has repaid its acquisition loan [1]. We also think a payoff below 1:1 is too thin for a stock that has fallen 50% or more three times since 2015. Across three horizons: the short term (1 to 3 months) is WAIT, because the tape offers no edge. The medium term (6 to 18 months) is ACCUMULATE only inside ₹2,070 to ₹2,150 with the ₹2,005 stop, or a re-underwrite after a confirmed Stage 2 re-base. That also needs H2 FY27 EBITDA to keep the ₹310 Cr floor on track and Research margin at or above 45%. The long term (3 to 5 years) is a genuine compounder candidate. The open debate there is price, AI price give-back and acquisition discipline, not whether the franchise exists.

Chapter 1

Start with the registration certificate, because it tells you what kind of company this is. MPS Limited carries corporate identity number L22122TN1970PLC005795 [1]. It was incorporated in 1970 as the Indian subsidiary of Macmillan (Holdings) Limited. For its first four decades it was a captive that served the group owning it [3]. Macmillan, a UK publisher founded in 1843, reached India in 1892. In 1976 the former British prime minister Harold Macmillan inaugurated a publishing-services division in Bangalore. It was long known as the Information Processing Division and provided editorial and production services to international book and journal publishers [3]. In 2004 the company added a wholly owned technology subsidiary, MPS Technologies, for digital-publishing work. A later demerger separated domestic Indian publishing, which stayed as Macmillan Publishers India, from the services-and-technology business that became MPS [3].

Two corrections to the commissioning brief belong here. First, the shares did not list in 2007. The annual report states they have been listed since 10 December 2001 on BSE and 21 January 2002 on NSE [1]. The company was renamed from Macmillan India to MPS in 2009 [4]. Second, I could not verify the label 'publishing's first outsourcing vendor' from any source I read. The record does support a narrower claim. MPS has sold offshore editorial and production capacity to Western publishers since the 1970s, which is a durable enough claim.

The second origin story is 2011. ADI BPO Services Limited, a Delhi business-services firm led by Nishith Arora, bought Macmillan's stake in MPS and took management control [5], [6]. The company itself dates the change of majority stake to '2011-12' [3]. That holding company is still the promoter. It owns 1,16,90,615 of 1,71,05,816 shares, or 68.34%. The holding was unchanged at the 31 March 2025 and 31 March 2026 balance-sheet dates [1]. The quarterly shareholding table shows 68.34% in every quarter from December 2023 to September 2026 [7]. I verified the share count in the audited note. I did not verify any family relationship between the promoter and the CEO, Rahul Arora. Mr Arora did tell the Q2 FY25 call he could answer 'from a promoter perspective and an MPS perspective' [8].

The holding structure has its own history. In 2017-18 the board proposed merging ADI BPO into MPS to remove a layer of holding company. A trade-press critic called it a regulatory tidy-up whose costs would fall on MPS [6]. The scheme was withdrawn in December 2018 [9]. The board revived it on 18 July 2025 [1]. The CEO told the Q1 FY26 call that the merger “will simplify the shareholding structure, enhance operational flexibility and strengthen direct promoter alignment” [10]. He added that this would be “all without affecting public shareholding” [10]. On 22 August 2026 the court-convened shareholders' meeting approved the scheme. All 1,16,90,615 promoter votes and all 5,51,213 institutional votes were in favour. Public non-institutional holders cast 1,52,327 votes for and 50 against [11]. The company's scheme page lists a tribunal second-motion order dated 16 September 2026. I have not read it, so final sanction is unconfirmed [9].

Read the right-hand column as the story. The business changed economic species three times. It began as a captive production arm (1970-2011). It became a US-and-Europe content roll-up with a platform sideline (2013-2020). It is now a three-market knowledge company whose margin depends on AI-assisted delivery and recurring contracts (2022-2026). Each transition was financed from internal cash, with one exception: the FY15 raise.

From 2013 the engine was acquisition, and the record is checkable. The company counts 'eleven acquisitions in thirteen years' [1]. I could price six of them from primary documents. Five carry no disclosed price in anything I read: Element, EPS, Mag+, THINK and HighWire. The table sets out what is verifiable and what the later record says about each. The pattern is the one the CEO described on the Q2 FY25 call. Look for a business with an obvious fix, and fix it fast. He called AJE “MPS 2.0” in that sense [8].

Total identified consideration for the four priced deals since FY22 (EI Design, Liberate, AJE, Unbound) is about Rs 322 Cr. I computed it by adding 42.09, 70.33, 69.67 and 140.40. Compare that with the Rs 460 Cr paid out to shareholders in FY22-FY25 alone. MPS has been a high-distribution company that acquires, not a company that raises money to acquire. Goodwill reached Rs 376.32 Cr at 31 March 2026, which is 40% of total assets [1]. That is the price of the strategy. The purchases must keep earning their way.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: What a captive's DNA does to a collector

A captive learns to be low-cost and reliable because its parent always has an outside quote in the drawer. It does not learn to be chosen. MPS's history is the process of acquiring, one deal at a time, the skills a captive never needed. Those skills are selling, saying no to a bad contract, and deploying its own capital.

The pattern repeats in the numbers. The two largest purchases by revenue pulled operating margin down. FY19 fell to 26% after Tata Interactive. FY25 fell to 29% with AJE, which was loss-making when acquired [15]. Each repair pushed margin back up. The CEO described the trade-off on the Q2 FY25 call. He said “typically you have to chase one of these two things”: improve the margin profile, or chase growth [8]. Our judgment: that is the honest summary of this business. Margin and growth have almost never been maximised in the same year. So H1 FY27's combination (revenue +17.8%, margin 34.3%) is the thing to test, not assume [2].

Now the business cycle, from the price-blind snapshot of the consolidated accounts [7]. I computed every figure here with Python from the snapshot's sales, operating profit and net profit rows. The annual reports prevail where they differ, and they differ only at the rounding margin. Over FY15 to FY26 sales compounded at 11.9%, operating profit at 10.2% and net profit at 9.9%. Operating margin ranged from 23.8% (FY20) to 36.2% (FY15). That blended number hides three very different regimes.

Era 4: FY25-FY26727 to 7685.6%29.0% to 30.7%11.8% / ROCE 41% to 39%AJE revenue cut by more than a quarter; Corporate Learning -16.5%; ex-AJE growth 15.4% [19]
Latest: H1 FY27448.5 (half-year)+17.8% year on year34.3% (EBITDA, H1)EBITDA +39%Research margin 48.2%; Unbound in the base; Corporate Learning margin about 25% [2]

The eras are separated by what was being purchased, not by the calendar. In Era 1 MPS was a margin machine without a growth plan. In Era 2 it paid for growth with margin. In Era 3 it repaired the margin and let operating profit compound faster than sales (18.5% against 14.5% a year). In Era 4 it pruned. The net profit CAGR of 26.1% from FY21 to FY25 includes a depressed FY21 base, caused by a 37% tax rate from one-off tax events [14].

Era 2 has a clean causal chain, thanks to the FY20 and FY21 reports. Tata Interactive lifted FY19 sales by Rs 96 Cr. It dragged margin down because the acquired e-learning business earned only a 4.4% EBITDA margin that year [12]. FY20 then went wrong for reasons management listed with unusual frankness. Journals lost volume to lower-priced vendors in a vendor-consolidation exercise. Two large educational publishers cut offshore prices and held back volumes ahead of a merger, with book-production prices down about 20% on average. A platform customer moved its IT work to its own captive [12]. Currency-adjusted revenue fell 7.8% to Rs 334 Cr. EBITDA fell Rs 11 Cr. FY21 reversed it. HighWire arrived on 1 July 2020 and platforms rose from 16 to 33% of revenue. Sales grew 26.9% even as e-learning fell 24.5% in the pandemic [14].

Era 3's gain is real, and so is its caveat. Operating margin recovered from 25.3% (FY21) to 31.3% (FY23). The e-learning units were restructured around a core-plus-contractor model modelled on Liberate [22]. But FY25's 33% jump needs decomposition. The CFO gave FY26 ex-AJE revenue of Rs 646 Cr, up 15.4% [19]. That implies ex-AJE revenue of about Rs 560 Cr in FY25. From that I compute that AJE supplied roughly 92% of FY25's Rs 182 Cr revenue increase. Underlying growth was about 6%. FY26's 5.7% is the mirror image. AJE shrank 27% (derived the same way) while the rest of the business grew 15.4%. The Chairman's letter says management 'deliberately set down a lower-quality book of business' [1].

Three structural changes ride on top of that cycle. First, the company introduced MPSLabs, its in-house AI and engineering unit. The FY24 report described it as a hub using machine learning, language models and cloud services for content structuring, generation and accessibility checks. It also stated a pivot towards AI-led SaaS revenue [15]. By FY26 the report cites more than 200 AI engineers and over one million manuscripts processed [1]. Second, Vision 2027, set in 2022, fixed the destination at Rs 1,500 Cr of revenue by FY28. The FY25 report called FY25 the halfway point [16]. Third, management reorganised reporting in Q1 FY26 from Content, Platform and eLearning into three segments. These are Research Solutions (60% of FY26 revenue), Education Solutions (27%) and Corporate Learning (13%). All earlier periods were restated [10], [1]. The FY26 report calls the integrated model 'one engine'.

Situational Scaling & Moat Twin: Genpact (formerly GE Capital International Services)

India and the United States, 1997 to 2008, with 2025 as the endpoint

Shared Mechanism: A captive services unit was created by a corporate parent to serve its own back office. In January 2005 it was released to serve outside clients under its own name, and it listed in 2007. The transition mattered less than the pace of non-parent revenue. The unit used the parent as an anchor customer. Meanwhile it built a sales organisation that could win clients the parent did not control.

Historical Numbers & Outcome: GECIS revenue was US$426M in 2004, all from GE. It became independent in January 2005. 2005 revenue was US$493M, of which 85% came from GE and 15% from new clients. It listed on the NYSE in August 2007. Revenue crossed US$1B in 2008 with 53% from non-GE clients. 2025 revenue was US$5.08B (source: Wikipedia company history, citing company and press reports) [23].

Direct Implication for MPSLTD: MPS is the same species at a smaller scale and a slower tempo. It was an in-house arm of Macmillan until 2011. Its largest customer today is 17.3% of revenue (Rs 133.17 Cr of Rs 768.37 Cr in FY26) [1]. Genpact took about three years from independence to get more than half of its revenue from outside its parent. MPS, fifteen years after its own ownership change, still has one customer at 17.3% of revenue. Its top five are roughly 45 to 46% [24], [2]. The lesson is not that MPS lacks a moat. The moat is relationship-and-workflow depth, which is slower to scale than a sales force.

Rs 651.22 Cr was returned between FY19 and FY25. I computed this as 56.11 + 93.08 + 41.58 + 155.96 + 34.21 + 128.30 + 141.98. That is about 99% of the Rs 659 Cr of cumulative snapshot net profit over the same years. FY26 broke the pattern. No final dividend was declared because cash went into Unbound Medicine. The FY25 final of Rs 50 a share (Rs 85.53 Cr) was still paid in FY26 [1]. The company's rule is that capital must earn its return within twelve months or go back to shareholders [1]. The FY26 decision shows the rule is discretionary on how 'earning its return' is judged. I found no special dividend or tender offer in the filings I read. The one buyback is the FY22 programme.

On leadership continuity the picture is mixed and worth stating plainly. Rahul Arora joined in August 2012 as CMO. He relocated to the United States in early 2013 to set up MPS North America and lead the first acquisition wave. He became CEO and Managing Director afterwards and is now Chairman and CEO combined [1]. His FY26 remuneration was 191 times the median employee and rose 31.33% [1]. The CFO changed on 1 October 2024, when Prarthana Agarwal succeeded Sunit Malhotra, who had joined in 2012 [1], [4]. The Company Secretary resigned in May 2026 [1]. The operating bench has turned over quickly. The voice of Corporate Learning on the calls changed from Archana Jayaraj (to Q1 FY26) to Rodney Beach as President (Q3 FY26) to Soma Bhaduri (from Q4 FY26). Sanjeev Talwar gave his first Education update only in Q1 FY27 [10], [18], [19], [25]. The CEO and CFO are the continuity. The layer under them is not.

Finally, the capital-allocation discipline in the acquisition record deserves a fair test. On the Q2 FY27 call the CEO said, “I would rather miss a deal than overpay” [2]. The record supports that on price. Unbound cost US$16.5M against a revenue run-rate of US$700,000 to US$800,000 a month, which is 1.7 to 2.0 times annual revenue (my arithmetic) [1], [19]. AJE cost Rs 69.67 Cr against Rs 52.48 Cr of FY26 pre-tax profit in AJE LLC alone [15], [1]. The record does not yet support a claim of clean integration. The Liberate goodwill impairment, the AJE revenue contraction and Corporate Learning's slide all sit in the FY26 accounts. The honest reading is disciplined on entry price, still learning on integration.

The chapter title promises the stock's past cycles. The business history above cannot answer that alone, because Pass 1 was written without looking at a price. This addendum was added after the business chapters were locked. The price history was pulled from the daily tape (unadjusted closes; weekly-close swings of 35% or more). The multiples are trailing-year P/E on the last reported fiscal-year EPS from the 10-year table. Data before 2012 is excluded because the Macmillan-era demerger and ownership change distort the series. The pattern is blunt. There were three drawdowns of 50% or more since 2015 (-57.7%, -67.5%, -51.9%). They happened inside a business whose operating cash flow roughly equalled profit every year.

The stock's own cycles since 2015: price swings, the multiple underneath, and the business event that explains each
Leg (weekly closes)Price moveP/E at the end of the legWhat the business was doing
Apr 2015 ₹1,022 to Feb 2019 ₹432 (46 months)-57.7%31.0x to 11.5xFY16 to FY19: sales grew from ₹257 Cr to ₹363 Cr but operating margin slid from 35% to 26% by FY19 (the last step being Tata Interactive's lower-margin e-learning work), and EPS stayed in a ₹38 to ₹41 band (FY15 ₹33.00 to FY18 ₹37.71, +14.3%). The multiple, not the earnings, did the damage.
Feb 2019 ₹432 to Nov 2019 ₹588+36.1%11.5x to 14.4xFY19 sales jumped 36% on the Tata Interactive acquisition (May 2018); the market paid for scale before the margin dilution showed.
Nov 2019 ₹588 to Mar 2020 ₹191 (4 months)-67.5%14.4x to 4.7xThe Covid crash on top of FY20's price cuts (about 20% on offshore book work), journal volume lost in a vendor consolidation and a platform customer moving work in-house (FY20 sales -9%, EPS ₹32.15 from ₹40.84). The deepest fall in the table and the cheapest multiple in the ten-year band.
Mar 2020 ₹191 to Mar 2025 ₹2,898 (59 months)+1,416%4.7x to 41.7xHighWire (July 2020) lifted platform revenue, margin recovered from 25% to 31%, buybacks and large dividends shrank the share count, and AJE drove most of FY25's 33% revenue growth. Trailing EPS rose 1.70x (FY19 ₹40.84 to FY24 ₹69.43). The multiple rose almost nine-fold.
Mar 2025 ₹2,898 to Mar 2026 ₹1,393 (11 months)-51.9%41.7x to 16.0xFY26 revenue grew 6% as AJE's clean-up shrank Research, Corporate Learning fell 16.5%, while, in the market's telling, AI worries weighed on content and services names. Trailing EPS still grew 25.4% (FY24 ₹69.43 to FY25 ₹87.05). FY26 EPS, reported in May 2026, rose a further 16.3% to ₹101.26.
Mar 2026 ₹1,393 to Aug 2026 ₹2,931 (5 months)+110.5%16.0x to 28.9xUnbound Medicine's first quarters, an FY27 EBITDA floor of ₹300 Cr (May 2026) and Q1 FY27 (+20% revenue, 34% EBITDA margin).
Aug 2026 ₹2,931 to 12 Nov 2026 ₹2,297-21.6%28.9x to 22.7xQ2 FY27 revenue +15% and EBITDA +27%, with the FY27 floor raised to ₹310 Cr; reported profit fell 6% only because the prior-year quarter carried a ₹13 Cr one-time gain.

In every leg the multiple moved more than the earnings did. Each multiple is the price over the last reported fiscal-year EPS, so the EPS moves use the same years. Trailing EPS rose 14.3% from FY15 (₹33.00) to FY18 (₹37.71) while the multiple fell from 31.0x to 11.5x. It rose 1.70x from FY19 (₹40.84) to FY24 (₹69.43) while the multiple went from 4.7x to 41.7x. From FY24 to FY25 (₹87.05) it grew 25.4% while the multiple collapsed from 41.7x to 16.0x. FY26 EPS, reported in May 2026, then rose a further 16.3% to ₹101.26. The business compounded steadily. The price paid for that compounding swung by a factor of nine. That is why the entry price, not the business grade, is the decision variable for this stock.

Read the table as a statement about the shareholder's experience, not the company's. A holder who bought in April 2015 was down two-thirds at the March 2020 low. That holder did not see the starting price again until 2023. A holder who bought in March 2025 at 41.7x lost half in eleven months while trailing EPS grew 25.4% (FY24 to FY25). Over five years to 12 November 2026 the shares compounded at about 26% a year. Over three years the figure is about 15% a year (price only). Over the last twelve months the return is roughly flat. None of that contradicts the audited record of Chapters 2 to 6. It explains why the same compounder can be a spectacular or a punishing holding, depending only on the entry multiple. Chapters 7 to 9 therefore treat the price as the open question that the business chapters could not settle.

Chapter 2

MPS reports three market-facing segments. The audited segment note (Note 36 of the FY26 consolidated statements) gives each one a revenue line, a segment result, depreciation and employee cost [1]. In FY26 consolidated revenue from operations was ₹768.37 Cr (+5.71%). EBITDA was ₹235.85 Cr (a 30.7% margin) and profit after tax was ₹173.22 Cr [1]. Behind that single line sit three very different businesses. Research Solutions is ₹463.51 Cr (60.3% of revenue). Education Solutions is ₹208.90 Cr (27.2%). Corporate Learning is ₹95.95 Cr (12.5%) [1]. The MD&A rounds those shares to 60%, 27% and 13%. The shares used in this chapter are computed from the ₹ lakh figures in Note 36. Management calls the common platform an ‘engine’ of content engineering, platform technology and AI [1]. What changes from segment to segment is who pays, for which unit of work, and how much of the unit’s value MPS keeps.

Research is the profit engine, with about two-thirds of segment profit on 60% of revenue. Education is the growth engine at +36.3%. Corporate Learning is the repair job: revenue fell 16.5% and segment margin dropped from 17.4% to 11.8%. Segment margins are the Note 36 segment result divided by segment revenue. The ‘EBITDA before unallocated costs’ figures add each segment’s own depreciation back (our computation) [1]. FY27 marks are management’s reported segment EBITDA margins and growth rates from the Q1 and Q2 FY27 calls [25], [2].

A note on how to read the profit columns. Segment result in Note 36 is struck before interest and before ₹29.31 Cr of net unallocated corporate cost [1]. On that basis Research produced ₹170.24 Cr of the ₹258.77 Cr total (65.8%), Education ₹77.22 Cr (29.8%) and Corporate Learning ₹11.31 Cr (4.4%), all computed from the note. Corporate Learning is an eighth of revenue and under a twentieth of segment profit; Research is 60% of revenue and about two-thirds of profit. The quality of this company is therefore mostly the quality of Research Solutions. Education is the growth engine and Corporate Learning is the repair job.

Segment 1: Research Solutions. The annual report describes it as AI-first knowledge solutions across the research lifecycle. That covers content and data transformation, research integrity and verification, author and editorial solutions, and platform-based hosting, workflow and analytics [1]. In practice there are five sub-businesses. First, journals production turns accepted manuscripts into finished articles. Second, scholarly books: management said it secured a multi-year commitment for 900 titles in FY26 [1]. Third, platforms. DigiCore and DigiCorePro handle production workflow. DigiCore launched at the Frankfurt Book Fair in 2012, is modular and open-source-based, and MPS uses it for its own production. Other platforms are MPSTrak (workflow tracking), THINK365 (subscription and order management), mag+ (mobile publishing) and ScholarStor. ScholarlyStats and MPSInsight provide usage analytics. HighWire is the Stanford-born hosting platform acquired in 2020 [26], [27], [1]. Platforms were 28.56% of consolidated revenue in FY25 by the CFO’s count [20]. Fourth, author services through AJE. MPS bought AJE from Springer Nature in February 2024, together with the Curie AI tool and Research Quality Evaluation, for US$8.40 million (₹69.67 Cr) [1]. Fifth, pre-acceptance services: the Journal Editorial Office and peer-review management that management says scaled because of AJE [25].

Worked example (Research): a top-five publisher hands over peer review and production. On the Q2 FY27 call, Christine Miranda said one of the five largest companies in the market is moving more than 110,000 submissions a year onto MPS’s peer-review platform. She said one of MPS’s biggest customers also gave it a large new production mandate plus a research-integrity mandate [2]. The customer is not named. The single customer worth 17.3% of group revenue in the audited note is also unnamed. We should not assume they are the same client [1]. The publisher’s problem is throughput. Paper mills and machine-written manuscripts are arriving in unprecedented numbers. As Rahul Arora (Chairman and CEO) put it on that call, ‘volume is not the issue, throughput is the issue’ [2]. What the publisher buys is a bundle of units. One is a screened, reviewed and decided submission. The other, for accepted papers, is a finished article. The build runs in steps. Submissions land in a peer-review system that MPS operates and staffs. Research Integrity Check sits on top of DigiCore. It flags paper-mill signatures, identity fraud and image manipulation before an editor spends time [25]. Editors and reviewers are assigned and chased by MPS coordinators. Accepted manuscripts then enter production. DigiXML converts them to structured formats, and AI-enabled composition and quality checks run. The Q1 FY27 call describes one-touch workflows, automated alt-text for accessibility and AI checks that catch errors before proofs. The output flows to a hosting platform such as HighWire or DigiCorePro [25], [26]. The price unit is the outcome: a submission handled or an article made ready. It is billed under a contract that the CEO says typically runs ‘three to five years’ [2]. The customer sees faster decisions and clean articles at a volume its own staff could not reach. The audited note shows how this reaches the P&L. Research employee cost fell from ₹185.13 Cr to ₹160.36 Cr in FY26 (computed from Note 36). Segment revenue rose 1.0% and the segment margin climbed [1].

Worked example (Research): an author buys help, and the publisher pays for the outcome. AJE sells straight to researchers off a price list. English-language editing starts at $13, scientific editing at $420, presubmission review at $289 and the Curie writing assistant at $11.25 a month. AJE says it has served more than a million authors in 192 countries [28]. The author uploads a manuscript. An editor matched to the field returns tracked changes and a report. The author then submits to the target journal. Since April 2026 AJE also sells pre-submission peer review. Christine Miranda called it the first genuinely new service in more than two years [25]. Management describes a mechanical change. Plain language editing is being absorbed by AI, through the Rubriq engine built on more than one million expert edits. AJE is therefore steering authors toward integrity and quality work, and toward being paid for results [1], [25]. The headline claim is a ‘manuscript acceptance rate of roughly 52% against an industry baseline near 32%’ in controlled work [25]. That comparison is management’s own data and has not been audited. Acceptance rates vary sharply by journal tier. We treat the claim as a sales argument rather than a measured moat.

What is weak in Research. First, reported growth was only 1.0% in FY26 because AJE was deliberately shrunk. Excluding AJE the segment grew about 17% [1]. Second, geography moved sharply. Research revenue from US customers fell 20.6% and from China 25.0%. Europe rose 26.8% and the rest of the world more than doubled (all computed from Note 43). That is consistent with the AJE author base in the US and China giving way to publisher work billed in Europe [1]. Third, dependence. One customer is 17.3% of group revenue and the top 15 are 58.05%. Management describes the large-publisher relationships as the heart of Research [1], [2]. Fourth, the publishers are building competing tools. Springer Nature developed a nonsense-text detector, an image checker (Snappshot) and an irrelevant-reference checker in-house. It donated the first to the STM Integrity Hub, so screening technology can reach the industry for free [29]. COPE’s forum also records that no AI-detection tool is 100% accurate. It adds that detectors cannot keep pace with new AI [30]. MPS’s answer is that it sits inside the production and review workflow and sells throughput, not detection alone. That is plausible. But detection by itself is a capability a large publisher can pull in-house.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: One profit pool, three different businesses

Put the segment note next to the call transcripts and one thing stands out. Research earns about 65.8% of segment profit, so when someone tells you MPS is a three-legged stool, they are being kind. It is one strong leg, one fast-growing leg whose newest component is lower-margin, and one leg being rebuilt.

That is not a criticism. A business with a 36.7% segment result margin in its main segment, and reported EBITDA margins of 45.1% and 48.2% there in the last two quarters, can carry a weak third segment for a long time. It does mean the diligence questions are narrow: how durable are the big-publisher contracts, and how much of each AI efficiency gain does the customer take back at renewal?

Segment 2: Education Solutions. The annual report defines it as AI-led learning content and knowledge solutions. These cover content development, multi-platform digital delivery, accessibility and learning platforms for education companies, ed-tech and continuing-education providers and academic institutions. Clinical knowledge now joins them through Unbound Medicine [1]. Inside the segment sit the OWL (‘One With Learning’) global education practice, learning services such as accessibility, translation and digital transformation, and Unbound [8], [1]. FY26 revenue rose 36.3% to ₹208.90 Cr, or about 29% excluding Unbound. Unbound added ₹11.78 Cr in its first 49 days [1]. The segment result margin edged up from 35.7% to 37.0% (computed). Geography is the key risk marker. 79.0% of Education revenue came from US customers in FY26 (computed from Note 43) [1]. In the first half of FY27 revenue grew 36% and EBITDA grew 14.5%. Education reached 30.6% of group revenue against 27.2% a year earlier. Unbound contributed ₹41.6 Cr in the half [2]. Unbound was bought for US$16.5 million on 9 February 2026. It had a run-rate of about US$800,000 a month by mid-2026. The CFO put its margins at 18% to 20%, up from 12% to 13% at purchase [1], [25], [2].

Worked example (Education): a nursing school subscribes to Nursing Central. The customer is an archetype rather than a named client. Think of a US or Canadian nursing programme or medical school, Unbound’s core institutional base [18], [31]. The problem is producing ‘practice-ready’ nurses with trustworthy references at the point of need. The product is an annual institutional subscription to Nursing Central. Unbound describes it as unifying clinical references, curriculum resources and teaching tools. Unbound Scenarios adds case-based clinical-judgement training, and AI Assist helps build teaching material. The references come from partners that include Johns Hopkins Medicine and Oxford University Press, both of which appear on Unbound’s own news page [32]. Delivery is a hosted platform and mobile apps rather than a project team. The CEO says Unbound is 35 people delivering about US$9 million of revenue [25]. It is priced per institution per year, with group discounts. Rahul Arora says that ‘every renewal with a customer, whether it’s a 10-year-old customer or a three-year-old customer, has a price increase’ [25], [32]. Unbound’s own release cites a 97% annual institutional renewal rate [31]. Billing is in advance. Unbound brought ₹34.98 Cr of contract liabilities (deferred revenue) onto MPS’s balance sheet at acquisition [1]. The customer sees an app its students and faculty use every day. MPS sees a renewal base. The AI answer layer is built on open large language models over a walled garden of licensed, peer-reviewed content. The moat is therefore the licensed content and clinical editorial process, not the model [25].

Worked example (Education): a K-12 publisher meets a state adoption deadline. The archetype is a US school publisher whose programme is up for a state adoption. One example is the Florida mathematics cycle that led Q2 FY27 bookings, with California to follow [2]. The publisher needs student and teacher materials, world-language editions and accessible versions by a fixed date. The CEO noted that accessibility rules stay on the books with compliance deadlines [2]. MPS’s education teams author or rework content and convert it to structured formats. They build accessible versions (EPUB, alt-text, remediation), translate and localise, and run quality assurance. AI sits in every step. The CEO said on the Q2 FY26 call that more than 60% of Education’s growth came from AI-powered workflows. He also said ‘the climb from 31% to 36% is all automation and AI’ [17]. Pricing is project-based, usually fixed-price recognised on percentage of completion, or unit-priced production. The revenue policy in the annual report names both [1]. The publisher sees a compliant package arrive faster. The economics depend on how much of the AI saving the publisher takes back in price.

What is weak in Education. Three things stand out. The first is lumpiness. The US adoption calendar drives bookings. Revenue growth slowed to 11.3% in Q3 FY26 after 52.4% in Q2 and 56.64% in Q1. Management called the dip a one-off [18], [17], [10]. The second is deflation. AI makes content production cheaper, so the same revenue needs less effort. An analyst on the Q2 FY27 call said organic growth in Education is ‘not very exciting’ once the acquisition is stripped out [2]. The third is mix. Unbound carries a lower margin than project work. That is the likeliest reason H1 EBITDA grew 14.5% against 36% revenue growth, after a 35.1% Q1 margin. We have not seen the Q2 segment table, so we do not put a number on Q2’s margin [2], [25]. Customer budgets in US schools and universities also depend on state and federal funding. Management spent part of the Q2 FY27 call on how US appropriations affect Unbound, nursing programmes and school districts [2].

Segment 3: Corporate Learning. The annual report defines it as technology-enabled workforce and enterprise learning. That means custom learning design and content, simulation and immersive (AR/VR) learning, and learning platforms and consulting, for corporates, government agencies and institutions [1]. The entities are MPS Interactive Systems (the former Tata Interactive Systems, acquired 2018), MPS Europa in Baar, Switzerland, and EI Design (2022). Others are Liberate Learning in Australia (2023, including App-eLearn Pty Ltd) and TOPSIM, the German business-simulation house in Tübingen [24], [1], [33]. The MPS site lists custom eLearning with gamification, simulation and animation, micro-learning and mobile learning. It also lists experience-centre and experiential learning design, consulting and learning platforms [34]. FY26 revenue fell 16.5% to ₹95.95 Cr. The segment result fell from ₹20.00 Cr to ₹11.31 Cr (margin 11.8% against 17.4%) [1]. The cleanup was structural. Liberate became wholly owned on 28 October 2025, releasing ₹15.73 Cr of deferred-consideration liability as exceptional income. MPS Europa is being folded under MPS Interactive. Rodney Beach, Liberate’s former promoter, became segment president [1], [17]. By year end MPS Interactive’s quarterly EBITDA margin was above 29%, and the segment returned to growth in Q4 FY26 [1]. In Q1 FY27 revenue was ₹27.60 Cr (+6.9%) at a 25.3% EBITDA margin, against 16.9% a year earlier, on a headcount about 33% lower. In the first half the margin was about 25% against about 16%. EBITDA rose 77% with 19% fewer people [25], [2].

Worked examples (Corporate Learning): a bank’s legacy training estate, and a German simulation. The first customer is an archetype: a large Australian bank or retailer, the kind of account Liberate serves. Management has mentioned a large banking customer and a major Australian retailer in AI-partnership work without naming either [8], [20]. The problem is thousands of legacy courses sitting in an LMS, LXP and SharePoint, disconnected from job performance. What gets bought is a programme, not a course. Soma Bhaduri described it on the Q1 FY27 call as role-based learning rebuilt from the legacy assets, AI chatbots for operational queries, multilingual delivery through the BridgeAI translation engine, and simulations [25]. Designers audit the estate. AI-assisted authoring, translation and quality checks compress production time. Media and development work is flexed through a gig and associate network instead of fixed staff [25]. It is billed as a fixed-price project on percentage of completion or as a managed learning service. The customer-visible outcome is performance at work rather than completion certificates. One verified data point shows how such deals grow. An invitation to join a conglomerate’s supplier panel turned into work above US$1 million plus a US$850,000 experience-centre project, per the Q1 FY26 call [10]. The second example is TOPSIM. A German industrial or retail group, such as the customers named on TOPSIM’s site (Kärcher, EDEKA, Vorwerk, Heidelberg Cement), licenses a business simulation for a leadership programme of ‘10 or 1000 people’. It is delivered classroom, blended or online on TOPSIM’s cloud platform [35]. Pricing is not disclosed. Management said in Q2 FY25 that TOPSIM’s top ten customers were under 40% of its revenue. It also said TOPSIM would pay its first dividend to MPS from FY25 [8].

What is weak in Corporate Learning. It lost 16.5% of its revenue and 43% of its segment result in FY26 (₹20.00 Cr to ₹11.31 Cr, computed). Its employee cost is still 46.8% of segment revenue, up from 44.3% (computed from Note 36). And its market is growing faster than it is. Rahul Arora told the Q1 FY27 call the corporate market grows 12% to 13% while MPS grew 6% to 7%. He called it ‘the area where a lot of the catch-up has to happen’ [25]. The headcount cuts and the move to gig delivery are cost fixes. Soma Bhaduri herself said ‘this is not a recovery of the legacy business’. She described a repositioning from learning creation to enterprise capability enablement [25]. The annual report records a ₹12.93 Cr goodwill impairment in FY26 exceptionals without attributing it to a segment [1]. The segment president called enterprise demand ‘patchy’ in Q3 FY26 [18]. Our judgment is that Corporate Learning has stopped losing ground on margin. Until it shows growth near its 12% goal with margins near its 30% steady-state goal for several quarters, treat it as an option on a turnaround, not a pillar [25].

Customers are almost entirely outside India, with 99.5% of revenue exported. The US was 42.7% of FY26 revenue and Europe 36.1%. Europe grew 25.6% while US revenue was flat and China fell 25.0%. Management’s own quarterly geography split used a different basis. It showed North America about 51%, UK/Europe about 29% and rest of world about 20% in Q1 FY26. The audited split is by customer location, so the two should not be mixed [1], [24].

Client concentration. The audited note discloses one customer at ₹133.17 Cr, or 17.33% of FY26 revenue (18.59% in FY25). Revenue from the top 15 customers was ₹446.00 Cr, or 58.05% (61.09% in FY25). No other customer reached 10% [1]. Management’s quarterly disclosures give a finer view. They do not always agree with the audited note, because the basis differs (quarterly against annual, clients billed excluding B2C customers). The CFO told the Q2 FY27 call that ‘our top five clients fell to 46% of revenue from 51%’. The client count had also risen sharply, which mostly reflects Unbound’s institutional subscribers rather than new project clients [2], [25]. The direction is toward lower concentration. But with about half of revenue from five clients, the business is still exposed to a single procurement decision.

Number of clients billed (ex-B2C)514 (Q1 FY25); 404 (Q1 FY26)About 840 (Q1 and Q2 FY27)Management quarterly; Unbound’s institutional base added from Q4 FY26

Concentration is falling but remains high. About 58% of FY26 revenue came from 15 customers, and the largest single customer was 17.3%. The audited and quarterly bases differ. Use the audited row for year-on-year comparison and the quarterly rows for direction [1], [24], [2].

What ‘AI-first’ and ‘paid for outcomes’ mean mechanically. The old production price was a rate per page, per article or per hour. Each hour saved by tooling was an hour not billed. The customer’s procurement team used the saving to ask for a lower rate at renewal. In the model management describes, MPS prices the finished unit. That could be an article made ready, a submission reviewed, an accessible title delivered or a subscription renewed. The saving is then divided between price and margin. Rahul Arora’s words: ‘Customers expect to share in our productivity, and they do, but we kind of make that up through more volume, and that’s where the margin kind of comes from’ [2]. In the Q4 FY25 call he said benefits are ‘shared fairly equally between the customer and us’ [20]. Management says 25% of revenue now comes from outcome-based partnerships and 45% from recurring themes [25]. Those are unaudited operating metrics without a published definition. The audited signal is the cost line. Group employee benefits expense fell 2.4% while revenue rose 5.7%. Research employee cost fell 13.4% (computed). Research’s EBITDA margin before unallocated costs rose from about 36.8% to 39.9% (computed). It then reached 45.1% and 48.2% in Q1 and Q2 FY27 [1], [25], [2]. MPS also capitalises almost no development spend (₹0.21 Cr of intangible assets under development in FY26). The work of MPS Labs, about 250 to 300 people, therefore runs through the P&L [1], [17]. What we cannot see is the rate card. Whether the price per unit has fallen, and by how much, is not disclosed.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: Separate the audited from the narrated

Every segment in this chapter has two layers of evidence. The audited layer is the segment note: revenue, result, depreciation, employee cost, geography, customer concentration. The narrated layer is the call: segment EBITDA margins, ‘outcome-based’ revenue shares, acceptance rates, run-rates, pipeline.

The narrated layer has been accurate where we could cross-check it: the Q1 FY27 growth rates the segment heads quoted (+13.2%, +42.2%, +6.9%) reproduce exactly from the Q1 FY26 presentation and the Q1 FY27 figures. It is still the part of the story where definitions are chosen by the speaker, which is why we lean on the audited layer for conclusions and use the narrated layer for direction.

What would change our view of segment quality (business triggers only). The first trigger is Research ex-AJE growth below about 10% for two consecutive quarters while margins keep rising. That would suggest price give-back is outrunning volume. The second is a top-five publisher moving integrity screening or peer-review operations in-house. The third is Education revenue falling year on year, excluding Unbound, in the second half of FY27. The fourth is Corporate Learning margin slipping back below 20% before it has returned to growth of about 12%.

Chapter 3

MPS sells directly, to enterprises, and almost entirely abroad. Of ₹768.36 Cr of FY26 revenue, ₹764.83 Cr was exports and ₹3.54 Cr was domestic [1]. There are no distributors or retail channels. Management describes a ‘captive base of 1,100 or more customers, many held for two decades’ [1]. New logos come through a central growth engine [1]. In Q4 FY25 the CEO described the commercial model as five prongs. The first is a revised go-to-market that sells firm-wide capabilities rather than individual products. The second is a heavier emphasis on ‘star accounts’, meaning cross-sell and up-sell. The third is new customers in every segment. The fourth is new capabilities such as DigiCore Pro. The fifth is fast integration of AJE [20], [10]. Relationships are executive-sponsored. On the Q2 FY25 call Rahul Arora said he personally sponsors the Springer Nature mandate. Springer Nature’s chief operating officer is his counterpart [8]. Selling is therefore account management at the top of the customer, plus RFPs when a customer consolidates its vendors.

Go-to-market, delivery and billing by channel (what the record shows, and what is inferred)

Institutional subscriptions (Unbound Medicine)Direct B2B sales to medical schools, nursing programmes, hospitals and societies in the US and Canada; group discounts; cross-sell with LiberateMedia, Pennsylvania office; about 35 people; run-rate about US$800,000 a monthAnnual terms with 97% institutional renewal and a price increase at every renewalBilled ahead; Unbound brought ₹34.98 Cr of contract liabilities at acquisition

Channels differ sharply in billing rhythm. Subscription and platform revenue is billed ahead of service, which is why contract liabilities (₹122.97 Cr) are almost as large as trade receivables (₹133.48 Cr). Project work is billed on milestones, which is why contract assets exist (₹58.30 Cr). Sales-cycle lengths are management’s qualitative statements where quoted; the record does not disclose RFP-to-award durations, and we mark inferences as such [1], [2].

How contracts are structured and renewed. The CEO told the Q2 FY27 call that research contracts ‘tend to be three to five years, tend to be long-term’. He said the lifecycle has not changed with AI [2]. The annual report calls the company’s defence against insourcing and client concentration ‘structural rather than contractual’. It points to relationships measured in decades, platforms customers run daily operations on, and switching costs [1]. We read that two ways. As an investor you get long contracts and sticky platforms. You also get a price reset once every three to five years, against a customer who has just watched AI cut production effort. The renewal is where the AI gain is actually divided. The record describes tenor and stickiness, not the legal form (master services agreement, statements of work, preferred-vendor status). Where management has discussed it, the vocabulary is ‘vendor consolidation’ and ‘global agreements’. In Q2 FY25 the CEO said the China presence lets MPS take part in global agreements with customers who had to compromise on their preferred-vendor strategy there [8]. In Q2 FY26 David Goodman, who presented Education, said MPS was succeeding in vendor consolidation [17].

How a new client is qualified. Large publishers and enterprises audit suppliers before handing over unpublished manuscripts, student records or learner data. MPS lists ISO 9001:2015, ISO/IEC 27001:2022, PCI-DSS, GDPR compliance and COUNTER 5 compliance in the annual report [1]. In September 2026 it added ISO 42001, the international standard for governing AI. Customers can now audit its AI use ‘the way they audit us for security’ [2]. The second qualification is the pilot. In Q3 FY25 management reported completing and delivering pilot projects with new logos [22]. In Q3 FY26 David Goodman, who leads MPS North America, said successful pilots with major K-12 and higher-education customers were converting into follow-on waves [18]. In Q4 FY25 management said the e-learning solutions being pitched were more complex and needed more iterated testing and piloting. That is one reason deals closed slower than expected [20].

Worked client win: from vendor-consolidation RFP to steady-state billing (the 2026 publisher mandates)

4. Hire aheadRecruit and train editors, coordinators and engineers before the volume arrives‘We are hiring ahead of these mandates’ (Christine Miranda, Q2 FY27); headcount rose less than 3% in Q1 FY27 while revenue grew 20%Margin drag in the quarters before billing

Steady stateAutomation and AI-enabled workflows carry volume with fewer touches; teams stabilise‘Our second half is about bringing them to a steady state while holding the margin as they scale’ (Christine Miranda); Q4 bills a full three months; FY27 EBITDA floor raised to at least ₹310 CrOperating leverage; this is where Research margins of 45% to 48% are earned
7. RenewalPrice and volume reset after three to five yearsNot yet observed for the large mandates; Unbound shows 97% renewal with annual price increasesThe real test of how AI gains are divided

The 2026 mandates show the ramp economics. MPS hires ahead of billing, which is a cost drag. Billing begins within weeks to months of award, and margin should recover as the work reaches steady state. First-half FY27 revenue was nearly flat sequentially, at ₹224.24 Cr in Q1 and about ₹224 Cr in Q2. EBITDA margin stayed at 34.3% in both quarters. That is what an investment-phase ramp looks like when operating leverage elsewhere protects the cost base [25], [2], [36].

A second, smaller win from the record is the HighWire platform. In Q2 FY25 Tony Alves, the senior vice president of product management, said MPS won a new logo away from its largest content-hosting competitor. He also saw ‘an influx of activity in new RFIs and RFPs’. His argument was that HighWire and MPS offer the only serious independent choice, because the alternatives are publisher- or private-equity-owned [8]. Hosting wins are slower and stickier than production wins. The customer’s content, usage data and subscriber logins move onto the platform. That is why the AR leans on ‘platforms our customers run their daily operations on’ as the moat [1].

Where the work is delivered from. MPS reports more than 3,200 colleagues in nine countries on four continents. It runs 15 delivery centres [1]. The primary Indian centres named in the annual report are Dehradun, Bengaluru, Chennai, Mumbai and Noida. The corporate note adds Gurugram. Production facilities are listed in Chennai, Noida, Mumbai, Dehradun, Gurugram and Bengaluru [1]. Pune does not appear as an MPS site in the record. It is the statutory auditor’s signing place. Outside India the MPS site lists US offices in Winter Park, Florida (MPS North America), Media, Pennsylvania (Unbound Medicine) and Princeton, New Jersey. It also lists Beijing for AJE’s Chinese entity, Singapore, Tübingen in Germany (TOPSIM), Baar in Switzerland (MPS Europa), Oxford in the UK (Semantico), Melbourne (Liberate Learning) and Woodbridge, Ontario [33]. Growth, sales and delivery leaders joined the calls from Austin, Texas, Noida, Bengaluru, Mumbai and Singapore [17], [2]. The AR describes a presence in smaller Indian cities as a cost advantage with a deep and loyal talent base [1].

The cost base is a payroll plus a variable layer of external contractors. Employee cost fell 2.4% in absolute terms in FY26 while revenue grew 5.7%, taking it from 45.1% to 41.6% of revenue; outsourcing (copy-editing, authoring, conversion, typesetting, media arts) rose to 14.9% of revenue. Attrition of permanent employees at the listed entity rose to 28.85%, which is a real execution risk behind the margin gains. Per-head figures use ‘more than 3,200’ as a floor for year-end headcount and are therefore approximate (computed) [1], [24].

The people economics have a second layer that is easy to miss. MPS Labs, the AI and engineering group, is about 250 to 300 people. That is separate from a platforms team of about 300 [17]. The CEO told the Q2 FY27 call that more than 200 engineers build the AI in-house. He also said ‘we don’t capitalize our expenses at all’ [2]. The audited cash flow statement agrees. Capital expenditure on intangible assets under development was only ₹0.21 Cr in FY26 [1]. That makes reported EBITDA cleaner than at companies that capitalise product development. It also means engineering investment is already inside the 30.7% margin.

Pricing mechanics. The annual report’s revenue policy lists three models. Fixed-price contracts are recognised on a percentage-of-completion basis using effort incurred. Time-and-material and job contracts are recognised on an output basis, such as units delivered, effort expended or transactions processed. Fixed-price maintenance is recognised straight-line over time [1]. Revenue is measured net of volume discounts, service-level credits, performance bonuses and price concessions. In FY26 contracted price of ₹772.80 Cr became revenue of ₹768.36 Cr after ₹4.43 Cr (0.57%) of such deductions [1]. Management describes commercial layers on top of that. One is per-unit production pricing (page, article, title, submission). Another is subscriptions (Unbound, THINK365, hosting). A third is AJE’s consumer price list, from $13 for English editing to $420 for scientific editing, with $289 for pre-submission review [28]. A fourth is a growing share of ‘outcome-based’ revenue. Management put that at 25% of revenue, with recurring revenue at 45%, on the Q1 FY27 call [25]. The group says pricing pressure is a ‘constant risk’. It handles that by trading price for volume and through automation [1]. We cannot see rate cards. The evidence for pricing power is therefore indirect: Unbound’s annual increases and the stability of reported margins while volume grows.

Billing and collection. The revenue note says trade receivables are non-interest-bearing and generally on terms of 0 to 60 days [1]. Trade receivables stood at ₹133.48 Cr at 31 March 2026, against ₹116.58 Cr a year earlier. They include ₹17.62 Cr of unbilled amounts. Ageing shows 65.9% not yet due, 18.4% under six months past due and 2.6% (₹3.58 Cr) over a year past due. The credit-loss allowance is ₹4.30 Cr, or 3.1% of gross receivables (all computed from the ageing table) [1]. Management’s own DSO measure was 51 days at the end of March 2026, 45 at the end of June and 38 at the end of September. The CFO said on the Q2 FY27 call that ‘DSO came down to 38 days from 45 at the end of June’ [25], [2]. The simple closing-receivables measure (receivables divided by revenue times 365) gives about 63 days for FY26 (computed). That equals the aggregator’s 63 days [1], [36]. The company’s own formula shows 51 days, and its definition is not published. We treat 51 versus 63 as a definitional gap, not a discrepancy.

Receivables plus unbilled contract assets less customer prepayments (contract liabilities) is only ₹68.81 Cr at 31 March 2026. That is about 33 days of revenue (computed), versus about 33.5 days a year earlier. Advances and subscription billing fund about two-thirds of the receivables book. But FY26’s contract-liability balance was lifted by ₹34.98 Cr acquired with Unbound. Excluding it, the balance fell from ₹98.79 Cr to about ₹87.99 Cr, partly because AJE advances were written back. Treat the customer-funded position as real but not growing on its own [1].

Forex: dollars and pounds in, rupees out. Nearly all revenue is billed in foreign currency. A large share of the cost base (Indian payroll, rent, subcontractors) is in rupees [1]. The annual report’s financial-risk note quantifies only the balance-sheet exposure. It says its sensitivity ‘ignores any impact on forecast revenue and expenses’ [1]. Net balance-sheet exposure at 31 March 2026 was ₹113.93 Cr in US dollars. That is ₹79.89 Cr of receivables, a ₹25.51 Cr intercompany loan and ₹9.13 Cr of cash. Euro exposure was ₹3.04 Cr and pound exposure ₹5.72 Cr. Other currencies were a net liability of ₹22.95 Cr, mostly acquisition-related financial liabilities. A 1% move in the US dollar changes pre-tax profit by ₹1.14 Cr. The euro moves it by ₹0.03 Cr and the pound by ₹0.06 Cr [1]. The operating exposure is far larger. US customers alone were ₹327.80 Cr of FY26 revenue. As an upper bound that ignores hedges and foreign-currency costs, every 1% rupee move against the dollar shifts revenue by about ₹3.28 Cr. That is roughly 1.4% of FY26 EBITDA (our illustration, computed). Management’s defences are hedging and planning rates. The risk table says the company freezes planning rates in two-year cycles and takes ‘adequate hedging’ [1]. The CFO said on the Q2 FY27 call that ‘because we plan the business at an exchange rate well below today’s, the new floor does not depend on the currency’ [2]. Forward contracts outstanding at year-end were forward sales of US$8.10 million for ₹74.32 Cr, an average of about ₹91.75 a dollar (computed). They also included £0.40 million for ₹4.85 Cr. A year earlier the figures were US$4.70 million and £0.50 million. The cash flow statement shows an unrealised mark-to-market loss of ₹3.65 Cr on those forwards [1]. Translation also mattered. The foreign-currency translation reserve gained ₹28.97 Cr in FY26. That flows through other comprehensive income, not profit [1].

The disclosed balance-sheet sensitivity is small, at ₹1.14 Cr per 1% in the dollar. The revenue-side sensitivity is bigger and is not quantified in the audited note. The forward book covers about 65% of the net dollar balance-sheet exposure on paper (rupee notional against rupee exposure, computed). It does not address revenue sensitivity. The real protection is that MPS plans at a rate it says is well below the current one [1], [2].

Of every ₹100 of FY26 revenue, ₹56.50 went to people and subcontractors and ₹12.80 to other operating costs. That left ₹30.69 of EBITDA. After ₹3.61 of depreciation and amortisation, ₹0.26 of interest, ₹2.03 of other income, ₹0.99 of net exceptional gains and ₹7.30 of tax, ₹22.54 reached profit after tax. Operating cash flow was ₹25.61 per ₹100, which is 83% of EBITDA and 114% of PAT. With only ₹1.10 of capital expenditure and ₹0.62 of lease payments, free cash flow after leases was ₹23.89 per ₹100. That is before ₹16.93 per ₹100 spent on acquisitions (mainly Unbound). FY26 profit before tax includes ₹7.64 Cr of net exceptional items. About ₹0.99 of the ₹29.84 of PBT per ₹100 is therefore non-recurring.

Reconciliation to the blind snapshot. The aggregator’s FY26 line shows sales ₹768 Cr, expenses ₹533 Cr and operating profit ₹236 Cr. It shows other income ₹23 Cr, interest ₹2 Cr, depreciation ₹28 Cr and PBT ₹229 Cr. Net profit is ₹173 Cr, operating cash flow ₹197 Cr and investing cash flow -₹132 Cr [36]. It ties to the audited statements. The aggregator’s ₹23 Cr of other income is our ₹15.58 Cr plus ₹7.64 Cr of net exceptional items (₹23.22 Cr). Its ₹533 Cr of expenses is employee cost plus other expenses (₹532.52 Cr). Its investing outflow of ₹132 Cr is the audited ₹132.87 Cr. That is dominated by ₹130.06 Cr paid for acquisitions (Unbound and the remaining Liberate stake) [1]. The aggregator’s free cash flow of ₹188 Cr equals our CFO less capex. The only differences are rounding and the grouping of exceptional items inside other income. One difference of definition matters for cash conversion. The aggregator’s CFO-to-EBITDA ratio of 103% is measured before tax. Our 83% uses cash flow after ₹46.66 Cr of tax paid.

Reading the waterfall. Three things stand out. First, FY26’s cash conversion was good in a year when it could have been poor. FY25’s operating cash flow was only ₹100.90 Cr against PAT of ₹148.91 Cr. That is 68% of PAT and 48% of EBITDA. ₹64.40 Cr of working capital unwound, from acquired AJE liabilities and customer advances running off. In FY26 that drag was gone [1]. Second, the business is capital-light. ₹8.47 Cr of capex is 1.1% of revenue, and software development is expensed. The uses of cash are acquisitions and dividends, not capex. In FY26 MPS spent ₹130.06 Cr on acquisitions (mainly Unbound, net of cash acquired). It paid ₹86.69 Cr of dividends and drew ₹42.00 Cr of term loans. Borrowings ended at ₹40.25 Cr. Management said it repaid them in full in August 2026, leaving ₹163 Cr of cash and no debt at 30 September 2026 [1], [2]. Third, the CEO said on the Q1 FY27 call that ‘the business is throwing out INR15 crores to INR18 crores of cash every month’. That is consistent with FY26’s ₹183.56 Cr of free cash flow after leases, or about ₹15.3 Cr a month (computed) [25], [1].

Historical Failure-Mode & Base-Rate Twin: Appen Limited (ASX-listed AI training-data and annotation services) (Australia, US and China, 2023 to 2024)

Shared Vulnerability: A people-plus-technology services vendor had a largest customer worth roughly 30% of revenue. That customer terminated its contract during FY24. The division that served it shrank by more than a third in a single year. The company recovered only because the rest of its book was growing and because it cut operating costs. The lost client did not come back.

Historical Numbers & Base Rate: FY23 revenue was US$273.0 million. About US$82.6 million of it came from the largest customer. That figure sums the four FY23 quarters in the company’s results presentation: 19.1, 18.3, 21.9 and 23.3. FY24 revenue from that customer was US$13.4 million. Total revenue fell 14% to US$234.3 million. The Global Services division fell 38%, from US$191.5 million to US$118.1 million. Revenue excluding that customer rose 16% to US$220.9 million. Operating expenses were cut 26%. Underlying EBITDA before FX moved from -US$20.4 million to +US$3.5 million.

How MPSLTD Compares Today: MPS’s largest customer was 17.3% of FY26 revenue, and its top five were about 46% of revenue in Q2 FY27. A one-client shock of Appen’s size (30%) is therefore larger than anything in MPS’s disclosed book. MPS’s large relationships run on contracts that the CEO says typically last three to five years, with platforms embedded in customer workflows. We have not verified how Appen’s contract was structured. The transferable lesson is about speed. Customers decide on their own procurement calendar. A services business with a largely people-based cost base pays for that decision within two quarters. Appen’s recovery came from diversification (revenue excluding the lost client up 16%) and from cost-out (operating expenses down 26%). At MPS, watch the top-five share. Also watch how much of the payroll can flex through the contractor and gig layer visible in the ₹114.21 Cr outsourcing line [37], [1], [2].

NARRATOR'S NOTE: Who funds the working capital?

Most services companies we study have a receivables problem: they do the work, they wait, and growth eats cash. MPS looks different because its balance sheet is partly customer-funded. Contract liabilities of ₹122.97 Cr nearly match trade receivables of ₹133.48 Cr, and the net position of ₹68.81 Cr is about 33 days of revenue. That is why FY26 cash conversion was strong even as the company grew.

The honest qualifier is that this cushion is partly acquired (Unbound’s ₹34.98 Cr of deferred revenue) and partly management’s policy choices (AJE advances were written back after a change in policy). If you are tracking MPS’s cash quality, watch contract liabilities excluding acquisitions, not just DSO.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: The cost line nobody headlines

The headline cost is payroll: ₹319.95 Cr, 41.6% of revenue. The second-largest line is almost never discussed: ₹114.21 Cr of outsourcing, 14.9% of revenue, paid to sub-contractors for copy-editing, authoring, conversion, typesetting and media arts. That is a variable cost layer, and it is where MPS’s ‘gig and associate’ strategy shows up. It is also the layer that generative AI attacks first, because it is the most standardised work.

So when management says AI is reducing effort, the first place to look is whether outsourcing grows slower than revenue. In FY26 it grew 9.7% against revenue growth of 5.7%, which means AI has not yet reduced the external layer. The payroll fell instead, helped by AJE offshoring and the Corporate Learning restructuring. That is a fact about FY26, and the next two years of the same line will say more than any call.

What is weak in the unit economics. We see four soft spots. The first is attrition. Permanent-employee turnover at the listed entity rose from 19.4% to 28.85% in a year. At that level, hiring and training costs and delivery quality become real issues [1]. The second is the ramp. New mandates require hiring ahead, and Q2 FY27 revenue was nearly flat sequentially for that reason. The margin held at 34.3%, but it depends on those mandates reaching steady state in H2 [2]. The third is concentration. About 58% of revenue from 15 customers means a single renewal negotiation can move the margin. The fourth is the quality of recent profit. ₹7.64 Cr of the FY26 PBT is exceptional, and ₹6.40 Cr of other income is a write-back of liabilities no longer required. About ₹14 Cr of FY26 PBT is therefore not operating profit [1]. None of these breaks the model. The unit economics are strong, with capex of 1.1% of revenue, free cash flow near 24% of revenue and no debt at September 2026. But these are the places where we would look first for a miss.

What would change our view of unit economics (business triggers only). The first trigger is free cash flow after lease payments falling below about 80% of PAT for two consecutive years. The second is attrition staying above 25% while the sub-contractor cost line grows faster than revenue. The third is contract liabilities, excluding acquisitions, shrinking for a second year. The fourth is a top-five client share moving back above 50% on the audited basis.

Chapter 4

MPS does not sit in 'publishing'. It sits in the plumbing between a researcher's manuscript and a reader's screen. Since FY26 it also sits between a clinical fact and a nurse's phone. The scholarly chain runs from author to journal (editor and peer reviewers), then publisher, platform and finally library or institution. MPS sells into four of those links. They are the author (AJE editing and pre-submission review), the editorial office (peer-review administration), the publisher's production line (copyediting, composition, XML, accessibility) and the platform layer (HighWire, DigiCore Pro, Think365) [1], [25], [38]. The payer is almost always the publisher, society or university press, not the reader. That one fact shapes the industry. A handful of very large buyers negotiate with a long tail of vendors.

Where MPS earns revenue along the research-to-reader chain, and what presses on each link
Link in the chainWho paysHow it is pricedMPS offering (FY26 / H1 FY27 evidence)Main pressure on that link

Read down the 'Main pressure' column. Every link faces AI, but in different ways. Production faces price deflation. The editorial office faces volume and in-sourcing. Platforms face lower switching cost. Clinical content faces chatbots. A single 'AI risk' label hides four different business problems. MPS's exposure is not equal across them.

Now the size of the pond. The STM trade association's 2021 Global Brief, quoting Outsell, put the global STM publishing market at $27 billion in 2018, $28 billion in 2019 and $26.5 billion in 2020 (a pandemic dip). Article output grew 5 to 6.5% a year over 2015 to 2020, while the count of journals grew only 2 to 3% [49]. Delta Think's 2026 update sizes the narrower scholarly journals market at just under $10 billion in 2025. That is up about 8%, helped by an open access recovery, AI licensing deals and currency. Delta Think expects growth to fall back to 5.7% a year over 2025 to 2028 [50]. Open access (OA) is the engine. The OA market was about $3.4 billion in 2025, up just over 20%. Some 52% of articles were published OA, but only about a third of market value [50]. In plain English, the system earns less per OA article but moves far more articles. An outsourced vendor's volume follows article count, not journal revenue.

Industry data points that shape MPS's addressable work
Data pointFigureSourceWhat it means for a vendor like MPS
Global STM publishing market (Outsell via STM)$27bn (2018), $28bn (2019), $26.5bn (2020)STM Global Brief 2021 [49]A low-single-digit growth market in value; the vendor grows by taking more of each publisher's work, not by riding market growth.

Springer Nature submissions and output, 2025Submissions up more than 30% to 3.1 million; 539,000 primary research articles published; over 53% OASpringer Nature [51]Submission floods hit editorial offices first; the published count (about one in six if the two counts are like-for-like) shows how much work is screening, not producing.

Management's addressable-market claimsMore than USD 800bn 'knowledge market'; education USD 416bn; corporate e-learning USD 353bn (2025)MPS annual report FY26 [1]Headline TAMs this broad (they include schools, universities and all corporate training) tell you nothing about MPS's realistic pool; I do not use them.

The honest picture is a slow-growing, concentrated value pool with fast-growing unit volumes underneath. A vendor wins when volume growth exceeds the unit-price give-back. That is the equation management described on the Q2 FY27 call. The outsourced content-services slice has no credible published size. I could not find one from a source I could open, so I do not quote one.

Consolidation of buyers matters as much as growth. The largest publishers, of which Springer Nature (2025 revenue EUR 1,926.4m) is one, are big, profitable and growing [52]. Management says on the Q2 FY27 call that 'the largest companies in our market are consolidating toward the partners that they trust the most' [2]. The top-15 customers took 58.0% of consolidated revenue in FY26, down from 61.1% in FY25. The single largest customer took 17.3%, down from 18.6%. These are computed from the AR26 concentration note: INR 446.00 Cr of INR 768.37 Cr, and INR 133.17 Cr [1]. Management says the top five fell to 46% of revenue in Q2 FY27 from 51% a year earlier [2]. Vendor consolidation cuts both ways. It raises your share of wallet at the survivors. It also hands the buyer an RFP at which every incumbent must re-bid.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: Count the units, not the dollars

When an industry's dollar market grows mid-single-digit but article counts grow faster, a vendor paid per unit lives in the gap. Suppose a publisher's output grows 5 to 6% a year [49] and unit prices fall 5%. The vendor's revenue is flat. Its cost base has to fall, or it is quietly losing. That arithmetic, not the headline TAM, is what you should keep in mind through the rest of this chapter.

Notice what the CEO told the Q2 FY27 call about pricing: 'With pricing as well, you know, Customers expect to share in our productivity, and they do, but we kind of make that up through more volume, and that’s where the margin kind of comes from.' [2] That sentence is the whole industry in miniature. The moat audit that follows tests whether it will keep being true.

Research integrity is the newest demand driver, and it has a body count. Wiley retracted more than 1,700 Hindawi papers. It began in 2022 with more than 500 tied to peer-review rings. Integrity became a budget line, and Wiley later launched an AI 'Papermill Detection' service with Sage and IEEE [54], [55]. Publishers then pooled their defences in the STM Integrity Hub [56]. Management argues this creates work because it 'sits on top of the production and peer review workflows we already run' [2]. Christine Miranda told the same call that 'The hard problem in research is no longer producing content.' [2] There is a counterweight. Springer Nature acquired Slimmer AI's science division to run its own checks. Integra already markets research-integrity and peer-review services to the same buyers [41], [57]. Integrity is real demand, but it is contested demand.

Education publishing and corporate learning are different animals. In education, work comes in waves. One wave follows US state adoptions: Florida's math adoption now, California next, per the Q2 FY27 call. Another follows accessibility deadlines that force every publisher to remediate content [2], [58]. The catch is that accessibility is also where every vendor now sells AI alt-text. That includes codemantra, S4Carlisle's NINJA platform and MPS's own automation. A fast-growing line may therefore commoditize quickly [58], [59], [25]. Corporate learning is large but cyclical and project-based. Skillsoft's Q3 FY26 revenue was $129 million, down 6%. Its Global Knowledge arm was down 18% and under strategic review [48]. Learning Technologies Group bought GP Strategies in 2021 and went private under General Atlantic on 31 March 2025 [60]. MPS's Corporate Learning is 13% of FY26 revenue (INR 96.0 Cr). Cost action repaired it, not demand. Margins reached 25% in H1 FY27 against about 16%. EBITDA rose 77% with 19% fewer people [1], [2].

Two facts stand out. First, MPS's closest scholarly peers are almost all private and disclose no financials. The only listed apples-to-apples reference is Datamatics, and its publishing arm is not broken out. Second, every named competitor sells the same AI pitch. So 'we have AI' differentiates no one. Management said on the Q4 FY25 call that its largest competitor grew from about $115 million of revenue to roughly $400 to 500 million over MPS's journey. The scale gap to the leader is real [20].

What distinguishes MPS is not that it does production at lower cost. It is that it owns a layered stack. At the bottom is production: DigiCore, launched at the 2012 Frankfurt Book Fair. Above it sits editorial and peer review, built on AJE capacity. Above that are platforms and hosting (HighWire, DigiCore Pro, Think365). Then, in a different market altogether, come clinical knowledge subscriptions through Unbound [42], [1]. Management says customers 'now buy three and four lines of work from us, when they once bought one' [2]. Its 'star account' programme lifted the average number of MPS business units per star account from 2.5 to 5.5 over two years [18]. That cross-sell index is the best single piece of evidence for embeddedness I have found. It counts integrated relationships instead of adjectives.
