Corporate Learning franchiseRestructured under Liberate Global; margins about 25% in H1 FY27 [2]2Project-based, budget-cyclical; the margin gain came from fewer people, not pricing power

My scoring averages roughly 3 out of 5. That is a respectable moat, not a wide one. The strongest moats are relationships and Unbound's retention, both 4s. The ones management mentions most loudly (certifications, 'AI-first', labour cost) are 2s and 3s. A durable moat here is relational and workflow-based, not technological. Technology is the part competitors can copy in a year.

Now the vulnerabilities, stated as plainly as the strengths. Per-unit price deflation is real. Scholarly Kitchen's November 2025 workflow map says production vendors are automating copyediting, typesetting and proofing with AI. Publishers 'are also evaluating and developing in-house solutions to reduce costs and vendor dependencies' [43]. Concentration is also real. Three customers each exceeded 10% of the standalone company's FY26 revenue. Together they accounted for INR 171.06 Cr, which is 39.0% of standalone revenue (computed from INR 17,105.50 lakh over INR 43,825.57 lakh). FY25 had two such customers [1]. On the consolidated basis one customer alone was INR 133.17 Cr, or 17.3%. In management's words on the Q1 FY26 call, AJE's B2B referral channel was 'entirely dependent on one publisher' [10]. The SaaS-style moat also faces academic challenge. A June 2026 Scholarly Kitchen panel included a Silverchair executive. It expects migration cost to 'come down quite a lot' as agents read and map data between systems [45].

Testing the central bear thesis: 'Generative AI collapses the price of content production, and MPS is a legacy typesetter'
Sub-claimEvidence that supports the bearEvidence that cuts against itMy verdict
Unit prices are fallingCEO concedes 'Customers expect to share in our productivity'; Scholarly Kitchen sees production vendors automating; RWS shows per-unit pressure in translation [2], [43], [67]Management says price cuts are recovered in volume; H1 FY27 EBITDA grew 39% on revenue growth of 17.8% [2]True in direction; so far overcompensated by volume and mix
MPS is mostly hours-based productionProduction and accessibility still carry the bulk of revenue; AI alt-text is commoditizing [58]Management says 25% of revenue is outcome-based and 45% recurring [25]; platforms and Unbound are recurringPartly true; I cannot verify the exact production share, which is a disclosure gap
Large publishers will in-sourceSpringer Nature bought Slimmer AI's science division; Scholarly Kitchen cites in-house efforts [41], [43]Top-5 publisher awarded a large new production mandate and an integrity mandate; top-15 share down to 58.0% [2], [1]A genuine, slow-moving threat, strongest in integrity screening, weakest in raw production capacity
AI-native entrants will undercutcodemantra, S4Carlisle and TNQ all ship AI platforms; open source models lower the cost of building [58], [59], [63]I found no AI-native vendor at scale in the evidence; the entrants named are established vendors; publishers move slowly on validated tools [53]Open question; this is the risk I can least rule out
The margin proves the thesis wrongH1 FY27 margin 34.3%; Research margin 48.2%, about 37% to 42% to 45.1% to 48.2% across Q1 FY26 to Q2 FY27; in Q2 FY27 Research EBITDA rose 25.26% on reported revenue growth of only 8.56% (21.71% excluding AJE) [2], [25], [17], [68]RWS held revenue within 4% of the prior year yet adjusted pre-tax profit fell 43%; a rising margin can also reflect cost resets (Corporate Learning had 19% fewer people) and planning-rate currency [67], [2]Supportive, not proof: margin is a lagging indicator of price, and the test is whether it holds through the next re-bid cycle

The bear thesis as phrased is half right. Unit prices are being negotiated down, and MPS does not deny it. The evidence does not show a collapse. Revenue and margins are rising together, which is the signature of a vendor winning share and volume faster than it concedes price. The thesis becomes dangerous only if two things coincide. Volume must stop compounding, and a re-bid must reset unit rates for a top-five customer.

My independent judgment comes in probabilities, not price language. Take the strong-form bear thesis first. It says per-unit deflation outruns volume and mix, so consolidated EBITDA margin settles below FY25's 29.0% within three fiscal years. I put the chance it proves right at about 20% [1]. The weak form says AI keeps deflating unit rates, customers capture a meaningful share of every productivity gain, and MPS has to run faster each year to stand still. I put that at about 80%, because management's own pricing remark concedes it. Next, the Research segment margin of 48.2%. I put the chance it holds within three points over the next eight quarters at about 35%. I put the chance it stays above 40% at about 70%. A 6-point gain in a year includes work still ramping to steady state and hiring ahead, which management itself flags [2]. These are my numbers, not company guidance. They would move on the evidence in the trigger box that follows.

Historical Failure-Mode & Base-Rate Twin: RWS Holdings plc (language, localisation and content services) (United Kingdom / global, FY2024 to FY2025 (machine translation then generative AI))

Shared Vulnerability: RWS is an outsourced content-services vendor embedded with enterprise and life-sciences clients. It is paid per unit of content, such as translation words and linguistic validation. Clients moved to machine translation and expected AI-driven efficiencies. Traditional translation volumes fell and unit prices came under pressure. The vendor's revenue barely moved. The profit pool underneath shrank, because cost base and mix did not flex as fast as price.

Historical Numbers & Base Rate: For the year to 30 September 2025, revenue was £690.1m against £718.2m. That is down 4%, or 0.7% on an organic constant-currency basis. Adjusted profit before tax fell to £60.4m from £106.7m, down 43%. The adjusted margin was about 8.8% against about 14.9%, computed from those figures. The group booked an £88.0m non-cash goodwill impairment and a reported loss before tax of £99.7m. Its own text attributes the Language Services trend to 'declines in traditional translation volumes as clients move to machine translation, and pricing pressure'. TrainAI data-services growth offset part of it [67].

How MPSLTD Compares Today: The earliest damage from AI deflation shows up in margin and profit, not revenue. A 4% revenue slip hid a 43% profit fall. MPS's revenue and margin are both rising, which is the opposite pattern. But this is the template for what failure looks like. Segment margin, especially Research, is the metric to watch each quarter, not the top line. The comparison is imperfect. RWS also absorbed a large integration. MPS sells platform and subscription revenue that RWS's translation core lacked.

Situational Scaling & Moat Twin: Wolters Kluwer Health (UpToDate and drug data)

Netherlands / United States, 2024 to 2025 (generative AI arrives in clinical reference)

Shared Mechanism: UpToDate is physician-authored clinical content embedded in hospital workflows and renewed annually by institutions. Its owner was not displaced by general-purpose chatbots. It migrated its largest US institutional customers to an Enterprise platform. It then layered an AI conversational interface (UpToDate Expert AI) on top of validated content. It also integrated with ambient clinical-note vendors. The workflow embed and the trusted content were the moat. AI became an upsell, not a substitute.

Historical Numbers & Outcome: In 2025 the Health division reported revenue of EUR 1,596m and organic growth of 5%. Adjusted operating margin was 32.1% against 30.3% in 2024. Clinical Solutions (57% of divisional revenue) delivered 7% organic growth in both 2025 and 2024, 'driven by good renewal rates for UpToDate'. By the end of 2025 most of the largest US institutional customers had migrated to UpToDate Enterprise. Expert AI launched commercially in October 2025 [65].

Direct Implication for MPSLTD: This is the best-case template for Unbound Medicine. Sticky institutional subscriptions plus validated content give pricing power through a technology shift. The scale mismatch is huge. Unbound is about $9 million of revenue per the Q1 FY27 call, against a EUR 1.6 billion division. The twin proves the mechanism, not MPS's ability to replicate it [25]. It also shows the tell to track: renewal rates, and the ability to keep raising price at renewal.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: What each twin teaches about where to look

RWS and Wolters Kluwer Health lived through the same technology shift and landed in opposite places. The difference is not that one used AI and the other did not. Both did. The difference is what the customer would have to rebuild. A model can regenerate a translation vendor's output tomorrow. It cannot regenerate a clinician's reference embedded in the hospital workflow. MPS has some of each. The whole question about the business is the mix.

Look at the one number that tells the two stories apart. Management says MPS now earns 45% of revenue from recurring themes and 25% from outcome-based partnerships [25]. If the recurring share keeps rising, MPS is moving toward the Wolters Kluwer side of the ledger. If it stalls, the RWS story is the one to study.

What would raise our estimate of business quality: (1) two or more further top-ten publishers awarding multi-year integrity or peer-review mandates alongside production work. (2) Unbound renewal rates at or above the 97% reported at acquisition, while annual price rises keep sticking. (3) The recurring-revenue share management quotes rising from 45% without the Research margin falling. What would lower it: (1) a top-five customer re-bidding production at materially lower unit rates without a matching volume commitment. (2) Research segment margin falling back toward 40% for two consecutive quarters while revenue growth slows to mid-single digits. (3) A large publisher publicly moving integrity screening or composition fully in-house. These are business-quality tests, not price triggers [2], [41].

Finally, the External Learning Hub. I would rather you form your own view than take mine. Everything in the hub was opened and confirmed to load as of 13 November 2026. I have said what each link is worth your time for. MPS's own pages are product marketing. Read them for what the company says it sells, then cross-check against the competitor and industry sources beside them. Two MPS business sites returned a bot-check page to automated retrieval: MPS Interactive and Liberate Global. I dropped them rather than cite what I could not read [69].

NARRATOR'S NOTE: How to use the Learning Hub against management's story

A good way to study this company is to hold two documents side by side. One is what management says on the call. The other is what an outsider says about the same thing. Management says 'AI follows businesses that sell hours. We sell outcomes.' [2] The Scholarly Kitchen says production vendors are automating and publishers are exploring in-house tools [43]. Both can be true at once. Your job is to find the evidence that tells you which MPS revenue line belongs to which statement.

The most valuable disclosure that does not yet exist is the revenue split between per-unit production and outcome-based or recurring work. Management gave the 25% and 45% figures on the Q1 FY27 call. It has not published the underlying definitions [25]. When you read the next annual report, look for that split first.

Chapter 5

Reported profit tells you what the business earned. It does not tell you what it is about to earn. For MPS the quantities that move first are not exotic. They are how much of each rupee of revenue survives as segment margin and how many people it takes to produce that rupee. They are how concentrated the customer list is, how much of the book renews without being re-won, and how quickly invoices turn into cash. I tested a longer list against the evidence and kept six. For each I give the baseline (FY22 or FY24 where a comparable exists), the audited FY26 figure and the latest reading (Q1 and Q2 FY27, results of 3-4 November 2026). I also give a forward threshold and the source. Note one trap. In Q1 FY26 management restated all periods into Research, Education and Corporate Learning [10]. Segment baselines before FY25 are therefore on the old Content, Platform and eLearning basis, and are flagged as not like-for-like.

Six leading KPIs for MPS: baseline, FY26, latest and forward watch threshold

Read down the 'latest' column and you see a business running at record margins on flat headcount, with concentration falling and cash converting. Read down the 'watch' column and you see where it can crack. Research margin is the profit pool, about 65% of FY26 segment EBITDA (computed from the segment note). A pass-through of AI savings to clients is therefore the single most consequential risk. Numbers marked 'derived' were computed by Python from the AR26 segment note (segment result plus depreciation).

Two data points in that table deserve a flag. Management's own numbers on Unbound's entry margin disagree. The CEO said on the Q2 FY27 call that 'we started off at what, 12%, 13% when we bought the company' [2]. The CFO said the first 49 to 50 days came in at 18.5 to 19% [19]. The concentration series also changes its basis. The Q2 FY25 call quoted a top-10 share of 48% [8]. The Q1 FY26 earnings deck shows top-10 at 44% for Q1 FY25 and 59% for Q1 FY26 [24]. I trust the direction of the largest-customer number, from 11.9% in FY23 to 17.3% in FY26. It comes from the audited segment note [15], [1]. In FY27 the AJE revenue contraction and Unbound's many small accounts then pull the top-5 share back down.

Raw PDF

In the snapshot, quarterly operating margin rose from 23% in June 2024 (the quarter AJE arrived loss-making) to 34% in both June and September 2026. Year-on-year sales growth peaked at +39% in December 2024. It turned to -2% in December 2025 as AJE was pruned. It recovered to +20% and +15% in the two latest quarters. That is the leading-indicator story in one table. Margin repaired first, and growth followed two to three quarters later.

Now capacity and structural potential. MPS's annual report cites a global addressable market of more than US$800 billion. It builds that from several pieces. Education solutions are about US$416 billion in 2025, heading for about US$878 billion by 2031. Corporate e-learning is about US$353 billion in 2025, heading for about US$874 billion by 2030. Research output was about 3.5 million science and engineering articles in 2024. A new healthcare-knowledge adjacency completes the list [1]. Those are company-cited figures, and I could not independently verify the two large market sizes. Aggregator listings I came across put STM publishing anywhere between about US$13 billion and about US$41 billion (search snippets; I did not read those reports). That tells you how soft such numbers are. The better evidence is volume. The STM Association reports that gold open access reached 44% of all global articles in 2025, with nearly two million articles. That implies roughly 4.5 million articles a year (my arithmetic). It also reports fast growth in research output from China and India [73]. Nature counted more than 10,000 retractions in 2023 as publishers struggled with sham papers and peer-review fraud [74]. The company's own report cites 14,000 retraction notices, a different count [1]. Either way, integrity screening is a real and growing workload.

The sharper way to size the opportunity is management's own. On the Q4 FY26 call an analyst quoted a slide saying MPS has only a 0.5% share of its total serviceable market. The CEO answered that the question is how to go from 0.5% to 1% and then 2% [19]. FY26 revenue of Rs 768 Cr at 0.5% implies a serviceable market of about Rs 1.54 lakh Cr. At the Rs 85.09 per dollar implied in Unbound's price, that is roughly US$18 billion. This is my arithmetic from Rs 140.40 Cr and US$16.5 million [1], [18]. The figure is about 2% of the US$800 billion headline. One percent of it is about Rs 1,536 Cr, which is almost exactly the Vision 2027 revenue target. The runway is real, but the quoted total market is not the relevant denominator.

Structural potential by segment: sourced market context, binding constraint, and what would relax it
Segment (FY26 revenue, share)Sourced market contextWhat constrains growthWhat would relax it
Research Solutions: Rs 463.5 Cr, 60% [1]About 4.5 million articles a year, gold OA 44% in 2025 and rising output from China and India [73]; more than 10,000 retractions in 2023 [74]Customers expect to share AI productivity: competitors market 30-40% lower operating costs for publishers [75]; largest customer 17.3% of group revenue [1]; US research-funding politics flagged by management [2]Three-to-five-year contracts [2]; one of the five largest research companies moving more than 110,000 submissions a year to MPS peer review [2]
Education Solutions: Rs 208.9 Cr, 27% (30.6% of group revenue in Q2 FY27) [1], [2]Company-cited global education market of about US$416 billion (2025) [1]; US state adoption cycles, Florida maths next, California after [2]Adoption timing: Q3 FY26 growth slowed to 11.3% against 52.4% a quarter earlier [18], [17]; federal and state school fundingAccessibility compliance deadlines; Unbound's 'recurring base it never had' [2]; more than 60% of Education growth from AI-powered workflows (CEO, Q2 FY26) [17]
Corporate Learning: Rs 96.0 Cr, 13% [1]Company-cited corporate e-learning market of about US$353 billion (2025) [1]Enterprise demand 'patchy' in Q3 FY26; legacy compliance work deliberately run off; headcount down about 32% [18], [19]Healthcare workforce learning built with Unbound (first joint sale reported); APAC logos [2]

The binding constraint differs by segment. Research is limited by price pass-through and customer concentration rather than by demand. Education is limited by US adoption calendars and public funding. Corporate Learning is limited by the quality of the book it kept after running off compliance work. In none of the three is the constraint 'not enough market'.

What caps the ceiling, then, is not a shortage of demand. It is three other things. The first is people. MPS has more than 3,200 colleagues and a Tier-2 India delivery base. It also runs a deliberate core-plus-flex model that lets cost scale with revenue [1], [19]. The second is client budgets. One customer is 17.3% of revenue. The CEO himself pointed to US federal research and nursing budgets as swing factors [1], [2]. The third is unit price. The CEO's framing on the Q4 FY26 call was that “AI is going to super-compress the price of commoditized production work” [19]. He added that this work would concentrate in a few scaled suppliers. The arithmetic of deflation is brutal and worth keeping in your head. A 20% price cut needs 25% more volume to hold revenue, because 1 divided by 0.8 is 1.25. A 20% cut is the size of the FY20 offshore book-production cut [12]. On the Q2 FY27 call the CEO said “Customers expect to share in our productivity, and they do, but we kind of make that up through more volume” [2]. That claim is the one the Research margin KPI above is built to test.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: Paid for outcomes, or paid for hours?

Management's strategic claim since Q1 FY27 is that revenue is shifting from effort to outcome. It says 25% of revenue is outcome-based and 45% recurring [25]. If that is true, AI helps MPS. The price attaches to the result, such as a manuscript accepted, a paper mill caught or a subscription renewed. Productivity gains then stay with the vendor. If it is mostly rhetoric, AI is the same price-cut cycle as FY20, only faster.

Our independent judgment: the margin evidence is real. Research margin went from 36.8% in FY25 to 48.2% in Q2 FY27 on near-flat headcount. But no audited disclosure defines 'outcome-based' or 'AI-attached' revenue, and the annual report gives no figure. The KPI to watch is therefore not the headline percentage. It is whether the margin survives the first contract renewal where a large customer asks for its share of the productivity.

Now the audit that matters most for how much weight to put on forecasts. What did management say, in its own words, and what did the accounts later show? I took twelve specific, dated promises from the earnings calls and annual reports. The Q4 FY26 call transcript is not in the standard library. I took it from the company's own exchange filing of 25 May 2026 [19]. A scorecard comes first, then discussion.

The target needs +39.7% a year for two years (computed); the CEO says it 'has always combined organic growth with acquisitions' [2]TOO EARLYAt the H1 FY27 pace (+18% a year for two years), organic revenue would be about Rs 1,070 Cr, leaving roughly Rs 430 Cr to come from acquisitions (computed)
12“we will not be giving the tactical forward-looking guidance” (Q2 FY25); “we no longer wish to provide any quarterly or short-term guidance” (Rahul Arora, Q3 FY25) [8], [22]FY27 EBITDA guided in May 2026 and raised in November 2026; tighter FY28 guidance promised at Q3 or Q4 FY27 [19], [2]MISSEDA policy reversal inside eighteen months. More disclosure is better for shareholders; the point is that the stated rationale ('no value in guidance') did not survive

Tally: two DELIVERED, three DELIVERED WITH COST, three PARTIAL, two MISSED and two TOO EARLY. The pattern is consistent. On numbers management can see (EPS, group margin, EBITDA floor) it has delivered or beaten what it said. On turnarounds and deal timing, which depend on third parties and customers, it has run late or short. Corporate Learning missed three separate timelines. The acquisition cadence has been slower than the pipeline language implied.

Where did management over-promise? In three places, and they share a cause. First, Corporate Learning. Management gave three different dates for the turn: margins 'here to stay' in February 2025, a 'banner year' for FY26, and take-off 'from Q2' in May 2025. The segment then shrank 16.5% with EBITDA down about a third [22], [20], [19]. Second, acquisition cadence. 'One or two a year' and 'five deals lined up back-to-back' produced one deal in twelve months [17]. Third, AJE growth. The thesis in November 2024 was strong organic growth. The reality was a planned shrink of more than a quarter [8], [1]. The shared cause is that all three depend on external timing (customers, sellers, regulators) and on a leader who is optimistic about timelines. Where management controls the variable, it has under-promised. EPS above Rs 100 was guided when 75% of the year was reported. The FY27 EBITDA figure has already been raised. The margin guide was exceeded.

The governance lens adds two cautions from the audited accounts. FY26's net exceptional item was a Rs 7.64 Cr gain. It nets Rs 29.68 Cr of write-backs against Rs 22.03 Cr of charges. The write-backs include Rs 13.95 Cr of AJE customer advances and Rs 15.73 Cr of Liberate liabilities. The charges are Rs 12.93 Cr of Liberate goodwill impairment, Rs 7.01 Cr of labour-code impact and Rs 2.09 Cr of AJE restructuring [1]. The Chairman's letter says charges were taken 'cleanly through the profit and loss account' [1]. That is true of the charges, but the write-backs offset them in the same line. Goodwill of Rs 376.32 Cr is 40% of total assets. The integration record in rows 5 and 7 therefore matters more than the headline growth rate [1].

NARRATOR'S NOTE: Floors, not ceilings

The CEO asked listeners to read the FY27 guidance “as a floor, not as a target we hope to reach” [25]. The Q4 FY26 call defended it as 'built bottom-up' and organic [19]. That framing is credible. The same management exceeded its FY26 EPS promise and raised the FY27 floor within six months.

Our judgment: treat management's numeric guidance as reliable and its qualitative timelines as optimistic. The one number that is a stretch rather than a floor is the FY28 Rs 1,500 Cr revenue target. By management's own admission it depends on acquisitions that have not yet been announced.

What would change our view of the business (quality triggers only): (1) Research EBITDA margin below 40% for two consecutive quarters while revenue still grows. That would mean AI savings are being passed through faster than volume recovers them. (2) The top-5 share back above 50%, or the largest customer reaching 20% of revenue. (3) Unbound's first full-cycle institutional renewal below 90%, against the 97% Unbound claims. (4) Corporate Learning margin back under 20%, or revenue falling again for two quarters. (5) Headcount growing faster than revenue for two quarters, which would end the operating-leverage story.

Chapter 6

This chapter audits the numbers behind every other chapter. We work from three records that disagree in small, instructive ways. The first is the audited annual reports for FY24, FY25 and FY26, all signed by Walker Chandiok & Co LLP [1], [16], [15]. The second is the Screener.in ten-year consolidated tables, reproduced below from our blind snapshot [76]. The third is what management told the earnings calls. Where they differ, the audited figure wins and we say so. Five differences need carrying into the tables. First, the aggregator’s Other Income line adds exceptional items to ordinary other income. FY26 therefore prints ₹23 Cr against an audited ₹15.6 Cr of other income plus ₹7.6 Cr of net exceptional gains [1]. Second, its Borrowings line adds lease liabilities to bank debt. The move from ₹4 Cr to ₹61 Cr is ₹40.3 Cr of real term loan and ₹20.4 Cr of leases [1]. Third, its EPS divides profit by all 17.1 million shares, including those held by the employee trust. It prints ₹101.26 where audited basic EPS is ₹102.11 [1]. Fourth, its Dividend Payout 0% for FY26 is a declared-for-the-year figure. ₹86.7 Cr of dividend, half of FY26 profit, actually left the company during the year [1]. Fifth, its total assets (₹912 Cr) net deferred tax assets against deferred tax liabilities, whereas the balance sheet shows ₹941.9 Cr [1]. None of these is a red flag. Each is a reason to read the audited note before trusting a screen.

Revenue compounded at 11.9% a year from ₹224 Cr in FY15 to ₹768 Cr in FY26. It reached ₹836 Cr on a trailing twelve-month basis. EBITDA rose from ₹81 Cr to ₹236 Cr (TTM ₹279 Cr). Net profit rose from ₹61 Cr to ₹173 Cr (TTM ₹185 Cr). The FY20 margin trough (24%) and the FY21 profit dip (37% tax rate, net profit ₹59 Cr) are the base for the later growth rates.

Read the ten years as three acts. In the first act, FY15 to FY18, MPS was a cash-rich, slow-growing content-services business. Revenue crept from ₹224 Cr to ₹267 Cr, about 6% a year. EBITDA margin sat at 32% to 36%. Financial investments were 56% to 64% of the balance sheet, so other income was 15% to 23% of pre-tax profit [76]. In the second act, FY19 to FY21, margin fell to 26%, 24% and 25% as acquired businesses and depreciation came in. Depreciation rose from 3.0% to 5.0% of revenue. The treasury was paid out to shareholders. FY21 net profit slipped to ₹59 Cr with a 37% tax rate [76]. In the third act, FY22 to TTM, the engine re-ran from a lower, cleaner base. From FY21 to FY26 revenue grew 12.7% a year, EBITDA 17.1% and net profit 24.0%. The 24% flatters because FY21 was depressed. The cleaner read is FY23 to FY26: revenue 15.3% a year, EBITDA 14.6% and net profit 16.6%. All are computed from the snapshot [76]. Audited EPS grew 16.3% in FY26, identical to profit growth, because the share count did not move [1]. TTM EBITDA margin of 33.4% is the highest in the table. It includes only two quarters of the post-Unbound cost base, so we treat it as a data point, not a baseline.

The bridge shows that the margin story is almost entirely an operating-cost story. Total operating costs fell from 74.1% of revenue in FY19 to 69.3% in FY26 (66.6% TTM). Depreciation, interest and tax barely moved from FY23 onwards. Other income was 23.4% of pre-tax profit in FY19 and is now minor (10.0% in FY26 including exceptional items, 2.8% TTM). Reported profit is now overwhelmingly operating profit.

Employee cost is the lever that moved margin. It rose from 42.5% of revenue in FY23 to 45.1% in FY25, the AJE and Liberate integration years. It then fell to 41.6% in FY26, even though Unbound joined in February and revenue grew 5.7%. Other expenses went the other way, from 24.2% in FY24 to 27.7% in FY26. Charge the ₹7.0 Cr labour-code cost and the ₹2.1 Cr AJE severance back to EBITDA, and FY26 margin is 29.5% rather than 30.7%. That is still half a point above FY25.

Three cross-checks on the bridge. First, the aggregator’s FY26 operating profit of ₹236 Cr equals management’s EBITDA of ₹235.8 Cr (30.7%). So the aggregator’s EBITDA is clean of other income and exceptional items [1]. Second, employee cost fell 2.4% in absolute terms, from ₹328.0 Cr to ₹320.0 Cr, while revenue rose 5.7%. Management attributes this to the cost reset in Corporate Learning (segment headcount down roughly a third), the AJE restructuring and AI-enabled production [1], [17], [25]. We accept the direction. But the labour-code charge (₹7.0 Cr) and AJE severance (₹2.1 Cr) were taken below EBITDA as exceptional, which flatters the reported margin by 1.2 points. The labour-code effect is a recurring higher gratuity and leave-encashment cost from here, not a one-off [1], [77]. Third, other expenses rose from 25.9% to 27.7% of revenue. Communication costs grew from ₹19.5 Cr to ₹24.7 Cr. Outsourcing grew from ₹104.1 Cr to ₹114.2 Cr. Rent grew from ₹1.5 Cr to ₹3.6 Cr. Royalty expense grew from ₹0.3 Cr to ₹6.3 Cr [1]. The annual report does not explain the twenty-fold royalty jump, so we flag it for the earnings-quality watchlist. FY26’s 1.7-point margin gain came from people cost, partly handed back through other expenses.

Only ₹4.9 Cr of FY26 other income (32%) is treasury income: bank interest, tax-refund interest and mutual-fund gains. The rest is liability write-backs (₹6.4 Cr), fair-value gains on the put-option liability (₹2.4 Cr), foreign exchange (₹0.5 Cr) and miscellaneous items. The exceptional layer carried ₹29.7 Cr of gains against ₹22.0 Cr of charges. The net ₹7.6 Cr gain hides a ₹13.95 Cr customer-advance write-back that rests on a policy change.

Two stories sit in that table. The first is Liberate. MPS agreed consideration of ₹48.1 Cr (including contingent consideration) for 65% of the Australian group in FY24. It booked a put-and-call option for the remaining 35% as a liability of AUD 4.14 million (₹22.3 Cr). The remaining stake was bought on 28 October 2025 for ₹5.1 Cr. The unused liabilities were then released: ₹5.9 Cr of deferred consideration on the first 65% in FY25 and ₹15.7 Cr of the put-option liability in FY26 [15], [1]. The same year the group impaired ₹12.9 Cr of Liberate goodwill. Gains and impairment are mirror images of one disappointing acquisition, so the honest reading is the net, not either line. The CFO told the Q2 FY26 call: “On a going-forward basis, this is a one-off. This is treated as an exceptional item because it’s a one-time entry.” (CFO Prarthana Agarwal, Q2 FY26) [17]. The Q1 FY27 call then confirmed there were “no exceptional items in the quarter” [25]. The second story is AJE. ₹13.95 Cr of customer advances in the B2C author-services business was written back. Management changed its policy to align with market conditions and revised commercial terms [1]. We do not call this wrong, and the audited contract-liability reconciliation shows it (reversals of ₹16.0 Cr against ₹3.5 Cr in FY25). But it is 6.1% of FY26 pre-tax profit and is not repeatable at that size.

That also settles the question the quarterly table raises. The aggregator shows other income of -₹5 Cr in the December 2025 quarter. It is not a foreign-exchange loss. Audited FY26 foreign-exchange items net to a ₹0.5 Cr gain. The unrealised mark-to-market loss on dollar forwards was ₹3.6 Cr at year end [1]. The aggregator’s other-income row includes exceptional items. The Labour Codes took effect on 21 November 2025, so that quarter carried the ₹7.0 Cr past-service charge plus the balance of AJE severance. That is about ₹8.7 Cr of exceptional charges against ordinary other income of roughly ₹3 Cr to ₹4 Cr [77], [1]. Our reconstruction ties to the year. Q2 FY26 had a net exceptional gain of ₹12.81 Cr, as the CFO described. Q3 was about -₹8.7 Cr and Q4 about +₹3.5 Cr, for a total of ₹7.6 Cr [17], [78]. We have not seen the Q3 quarterly filing, so the split is our arithmetic. The annual total is audited. The practical lesson is to check exceptional items before suspecting currency when a screen shows a negative other-income quarter.

Raw PDF

Quarterly revenue rose from ₹130 Cr (Sep 2023) to ₹224 Cr (Sep 2026). EBITDA margin rose from 31% to 34%, with the June 2024 quarter (23%) as the AJE-dilution low. Other income swings between -₹5 Cr (Dec 2025) and +₹15 Cr (Sep 2025) because exceptional items are included. Quarterly tax rates range from 20% to 29%.

Three features of the quarterly run matter for earnings quality. First, margin has stepped up in stages rather than drifting. It was 23% in June 2024, 27% in June 2025, 33% in March 2026 and 34% in June and September 2026. That fits a cost reset plus the AI-productivity story management tells [76], [2]. Second, profit growth lags EBITDA growth in the first half of FY27. EBITDA rose about 39% (₹154 Cr) but profit after tax only 13% (₹102 Cr against ₹90 Cr). The September 2025 quarter carried the ₹12.8 Cr Liberate gain, and tax rates reverted from 20% to 25% [2], [76]. Management itself said that, set aside that gain, profit before tax grew 23.1% in the quarter and 33.9% in the half [2]. Third, the March 2026 quarter (revenue ₹205 Cr, EBITDA ₹68 Cr) is the first to include Unbound. Unbound contributed ₹11.8 Cr of revenue in its first 49 days. According to the CFO its EBITDA margin was roughly 18.5% to 19% [1], [78]. That is well below the group’s 30%-plus, so Unbound dilutes margin until it is integrated. The first-half FY27 margin of 34.3% shows the dilution is small.

Conversion is high but not smooth. CFO/PAT ranged from 0.64x (FY17) to 1.68x (FY21), with weak years in FY19 (0.71x) and FY25 (0.68x). Cumulatively the ten years FY17 to FY26 convert profit to cash at 1.00x, and CFO is 72% of EBITDA after tax. A single year is noise. The decade is the evidence.

Of the ₹95.9 Cr rise in operating cash flow, ₹65.0 Cr is the swing in working capital. That was a ₹64.4 Cr drain in FY25 against a ₹0.6 Cr inflow in FY26. The other ₹28.0 Cr is a genuine increase in operating cash flow before working capital. Capital expenditure is tiny, about 1.1% of revenue. Free cash flow after lease payments rose from ₹87.3 Cr to ₹183.6 Cr, or 1.06x profit in FY26 against 0.59x in FY25.

The jump is real, but it is mostly a reversal of a one-time drain, so we do not extrapolate it. In FY25 the company settled liabilities that were unusually high at the March 2024 year end, after an acquisition-heavy FY24. The ‘others’ line within other current liabilities fell from ₹32.5 Cr to ₹0.9 Cr. Employee payables fell from ₹30.2 Cr to ₹12.3 Cr. Contract liabilities were flat at about ₹98 Cr. The annual report does not itemise those balances. In FY26 the same cash-flow lines turned mildly positive [1], [16]. Operating cash flow before working capital was ₹242.9 Cr, which is 103% of EBITDA. Cash taxes were only 20.3% of pre-tax profit (₹46.7 Cr), below the 24.5% book rate [1]. In other words, FY26 was a year with essentially zero working-capital drag. The average drag over FY23 to FY26 was ₹24 Cr a year (₹16.4 Cr, ₹17.0 Cr, ₹64.4 Cr and a ₹0.6 Cr inflow). A growing business also ties up cash. Receivables plus contract assets run at 91 days of revenue, so each extra ₹100 Cr of revenue absorbs about ₹25 Cr before offsetting advances [1], [15]. Management’s own framing is consistent with this. The CEO told the Q1 FY27 call: “The business is throwing out INR15 crores to INR18 crores of cash every month” and “Cash flow is normally fairly similar to PAT.” (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q1 FY27) [25]. That is ₹180 Cr to ₹216 Cr a year, which brackets FY26’s ₹197 Cr. Our decade-long conversion of 1.00x supports it. The first half of FY27 points the same way. Cash and liquid investments rose from ₹113.75 Cr to ₹163 Cr while ₹40.25 Cr of debt was repaid and no dividend was paid. That is about ₹89.5 Cr of free cash on ₹102 Cr of profit, by our arithmetic and ignoring currency and small items [1], [2].

Receivable quality is high. Only ₹4.1 Cr, 3.0% of gross receivables, is more than six months past due, and the ₹4.3 Cr credit-loss allowance covers it. What changed in FY26 is the liability side. Customer-funded liabilities rose from ₹98.8 Cr to ₹123.0 Cr, but ₹35.0 Cr of that arrived with Unbound. Excluding Unbound’s acquired balances, our measure of operating working capital would have risen from 25 to 30 days of revenue, not fallen to 11.

That table explains the swing in the aggregator’s working-capital days from +24 to -4. When MPS acquires a subscription or advance-billing business, it inherits the customer’s prepayment on the liability side but not the cash. Unbound arrived with ₹35.0 Cr of contract liabilities and ₹16.5 Cr of trade payables. Against these it brought only ₹3.4 Cr of receivables and ₹9.6 Cr of other current assets. That is a net working-capital balance of -₹40.3 Cr, or 19 days of FY26 revenue [1]. The aggregator’s table shows the same pattern in FY24, the year AJE arrived with ₹72.3 Cr of other current liabilities. Working-capital days dropped to -13 and then snapped back to +24 in FY25 [76], [1]. Acquired deferred revenue is borrowed from the future. The cash arrived before MPS owned the business, and the service is delivered afterwards. For a steady subscription renewal book like Unbound (company filings cite about 97% gross retention) the balance renews rather than unwinds [79]. If renewals slipped, the same ₹35 Cr would be recognised as revenue without new cash. At AJE the evidence is mixed. Contract liabilities were flat in FY25 (₹98.2 Cr to ₹98.8 Cr). AJE’s customer advances fell from ₹40.8 Cr to ₹26.2 Cr in FY26, of which ₹14.0 Cr was the write-back rather than a cash outflow [16], [1]. The organic signal is the rise in receivables plus contract assets from 83 to 91 days. Ordinary working-capital absorption is the real FY26 story, not the aggregator’s -4. Management’s reported DSO (51 days at March) falls to 45 at June and 38 at September. Our 63 days uses full-year revenue and includes unbilled receivables. We can reproduce about 51 only by excluding unbilled and annualising the fourth quarter. The CFO’s Q2 FY27 summary: “The earnings are also turning into cash. DSO came down to 38 days from 45 at the end of June.” (CFO Prarthana Agarwal, Q2 FY27) [2].

NARRATOR'S NOTE: cash conversion is a decade statistic, not a year statistic

Our house habit is to judge cash conversion over at least five years. A single year can be flattered or damaged by what other people prepaid or what you prepaid for them. MPS makes the point cleanly. FY25 looks poor (0.68x of profit) and FY26 looks excellent (1.14x). Together the two years convert 0.90x of profit into cash, while the ten years to FY26 convert 1.00x. The difference between the two years is not performance. It is a ₹64 Cr drain from settling balances left over from an acquisition-heavy FY24, against a flat working-capital year.

So when the aggregator headline says CFO jumped 95%, our reflex is to ask what the other side of the entry was. Here the answer is benign. We found no pull-forward of receipts, no stretched payables (trade payables are only ₹42.7 Cr), no receivable factoring, and capital expenditure of 1.1% of revenue. That is why we trust the decade number more than either year. The residual risk is not that profit is not cash. It is that acquisition accounting and exceptional items sit between profit and cash, which is where the rest of this chapter looks.

Equity capital has been flat at ₹17 Cr. Reserves rose from ₹237 Cr (FY15) to ₹579 Cr (Mar 2026) and ₹686 Cr (Sep 2026). Borrowings of ₹61 Cr in March 2026 are ₹40.3 Cr of term loan plus ₹20.4 Cr of leases. By September 2026 the loan has been repaid and ₹18 Cr is leases only. Fixed assets of ₹540 Cr are 92% goodwill and acquired intangibles.

Two structural changes define the balance sheet. The first happened between FY18 and FY21. Financial investments fell from ₹288 Cr to ₹12 Cr as the cash pile was returned to shareholders (₹93 Cr in FY20, ₹156 Cr including a ₹104.6 Cr buyback in FY22) and used for acquisitions [76], [1]. The second is the build-up of goodwill and acquired intangibles. Fixed assets rose from ₹173 Cr in FY23 to ₹388 Cr in FY24 on AJE and Liberate, and to ₹540 Cr in FY26 on Unbound. At 31 March 2026 audited goodwill was ₹376.3 Cr, or 40.0% of total assets of ₹941.9 Cr. Other intangibles were ₹117.8 Cr. Together they are 52.5% of total assets and 83% of the ₹596.3 Cr equity. Tangible net worth (equity less goodwill and intangibles) fell from ₹162.1 Cr to ₹102.2 Cr during the year, even though profit was ₹173.2 Cr. Goodwill and intangibles grew by ₹177.8 Cr (Unbound plus ₹21.5 Cr of currency uplift on foreign-currency goodwill), while equity grew by only ₹117.9 Cr after ₹86.7 Cr of dividends [1]. A quarter of the ₹117.9 Cr equity increase (₹29.0 Cr) is the foreign-currency translation reserve, which is non-cash. That is the biggest single reason we prefer cash-flow measures over balance-sheet measures for this company.

The company is net cash on every measure we can build. Management-defined cash is ₹113.7 Cr against ₹40.3 Cr of bank debt, leaving ₹73.5 Cr net, or ₹53.1 Cr after ₹20.4 Cr of lease liabilities. The balance sheet at March 2026 is less liquid than a year earlier, with a current ratio of 1.45 against 1.97. The gratuity provision rose from ₹2.1 Cr to ₹9.2 Cr (labour code). Current liabilities also hold ₹10.5 Cr of loan instalments, ₹14.3 Cr of deferred consideration and Unbound’s customer prepayments.

MPS paid US$16.5 million (₹140.4 Cr). It recognised ₹123.9 Cr of goodwill (88% of the price) and ₹52.3 Cr of identified intangibles. Identifiable net assets were ₹16.5 Cr, after ₹35.0 Cr of customer prepayments. The price is about 2.1 times Unbound’s pro forma annual revenue of ₹67.1 Cr and about 23.6 times its pro forma annual profit after tax of ₹5.9 Cr. The purchase-price allocation is provisional. The audit report notes that MPS used the measurement-period exemption, so the numbers can change until February 2027.

How it was funded matters as much as what was paid. On 30 January 2026 MPS North America LLC signed the Stock Purchase Agreement. On 4 February the company disclosed the funding: internal accruals, inter-company loans of US$4.94 million, an INR 420 million ICICI Bank term loan at 7.85% a year, and a US$9.81 million equity infusion into the US subsidiary [80], [79]. The term loan was ₹42 Cr for four years, linked to the one-month marginal-cost lending rate and secured on current assets and movable fixed assets. Its covenants cover debt-to-EBITDA, debt-to-net-worth and debt-service cover, and the audit report says they were complied with [1]. FY26 interest on it was only ₹0.49 Cr. It stood at ₹40.25 Cr in March and ₹37.63 Cr in June. In the CFO’s words: “In August, we repaid in full the borrowings we took for Unbound Medicine, so we carry zero debt, and we held INR163 crores of cash as on 30th September.” (CFO Prarthana Agarwal, Q2 FY27) [2]. The CEO has positioned this as a template: “We paid about INR70 crores for AJE. AJE earns more than half of that back every year now” and “Unbound is on the same path.” (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q2 FY27) [2]. For AJE the audited facts are that the price was US$8.4 million (₹69.8 Cr). The provisional goodwill of ₹101.7 Cr was cut 28% to ₹73.0 Cr when the measurement period closed, mostly by recognising a ₹23.6 Cr deferred tax asset [1], [81]. That history says provisional goodwill moves, so a reader should expect Unbound’s ₹123.9 Cr to move too.

Source or use₹ Cr

Capital expenditure (including software and intangibles under development)-8.5

Operating cash flow of ₹196.8 Cr funded ₹130.1 Cr of acquisitions (Unbound net of cash acquired, plus the remaining 35% of Liberate). It also funded ₹86.7 Cr of dividends (the FY25 final of ₹50 a share), capital expenditure and leases. Net new borrowing of ₹40.25 Cr bridged the gap. Cash and equivalents rose ₹12.4 Cr to ₹76.6 Cr, plus ₹0.7 Cr of currency effect.

The balance-sheet reading is favourable, with two qualifiers. First the favourable side. Debt-to-equity was 0.07 and the debt-service cover ratio the company reports is 28.9 times. The term loan was fully repaid within six months of drawing. That is the behaviour of a cash-rich buyer using a facility for timing, not leverage [1], [2]. Qualifier one: the capital-structure note says the group has ‘always been a net cash group’. But management has also said it is comfortable with debt well above this level. The CEO told the Q1 FY27 call that an acquisition “in the INR300 crores, INR500 crores range seems fairly doable between internal cash accruals as well as debt”. He added that debt of ₹200 Cr is ‘very comfortable’ against profit of ₹200 Cr (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q1 FY27) [25]. The near-zero debt balance is a feature of the last two years, not a permanent policy. Qualifier two: lease liabilities rose five-fold to ₹20.4 Cr. That includes ₹19.6 Cr of new leases in the year and ₹3.5 Cr acquired with Unbound. The group also expensed ₹17.1 Cr on short-term leases. So ‘no debt’ understates fixed premises commitments modestly, although at 0.26 times EBITDA (loan plus leases) they are small [1]. Currency is the other structural exposure. 99.5% of revenue is earned abroad. The net dollar balance-sheet exposure was ₹113.9 Cr, and a 1% move changes pre-tax profit by ₹1.1 Cr. The forwards in place covered US$8.1 million (₹74.3 Cr) at an average ₹91.75 per dollar, about 65% of that net exposure. Management says it plans at a deliberately conservative rate and freezes planning rates in two-year cycles [1], [2].

Goodwill by cash-generating unit and the earnings it must justify (audited, 31 March 2026, ₹ Cr)

Corporate Learning113.3100.496.011.311.8%8.9x
Total243.9376.3768.4258.833.7%1.5x

Research goodwill (₹119.5 Cr) equals 0.7 times its segment result and Education goodwill (₹156.4 Cr) 2.0 times. Corporate Learning goodwill (₹100.4 Cr) is 8.9 times a segment result that fell 43% to ₹11.3 Cr. That is where the single FY26 impairment (₹12.9 Cr on Liberate) landed. It is the unit to watch.

Impairment testing is done annually on value in use, with external fair-value experts. The discount rates are 14.5% to 19% and terminal growth is 1.5% to 4%. The audited note says there would be no impairment even if the cost of capital rose one point and terminal growth fell one point [1]. Walker Chandiok made goodwill impairment a key audit matter in FY26, for the first time in the three reports we read. Liberate was the unit that failed the test [1], [16], [15]. We read the Corporate Learning figures with care. Revenue fell 16.5% to ₹96.0 Cr and segment result fell from ₹20.0 Cr to ₹11.3 Cr. The ₹100.4 Cr of goodwill has not been written down beyond Liberate. In its favour, the segment’s EBITDA margin has been recovering. The Q2 FY27 call put it at about 16% rising to 25% in the first half of FY27, with 19% fewer people. Rodney Beach, the Australian investor who put ₹8.7 Cr into MPS Interactive Systems in October 2025, has become president of the segment [2], [1]. Our judgement is that no further impairment is evident from the audited headroom statement. But a segment earning ₹11 Cr to carry ₹100 Cr of goodwill needs the margin recovery to be real. The FY26 P&L carries a non-cash impairment and a larger non-cash gain on the related liability in the same year. That is why we net the two.

ROCE decomposition: aggregator figure, our simple calculation and the audited note (EBIT = EBITDA less depreciation; capital = equity plus borrowings)
MeasureFY15FY19FY20FY22FY25FY26
Aggregator ROCE49%21%17%31%41%39%
Our EBIT margin (EBITDA less depreciation)33.9%22.9%19.3%23.4%25.3%27.1%
Revenue / (equity plus borrowings), times0.880.770.861.181.511.17
Our simple ROCE29.7%17.6%16.6%27.7%38.2%31.7%
Financial investments as % of total assets56%39%19%1%3%3%
Net worth (₹ Cr)256472367367478596
Audited note: EBIT over average capital employed (incl. leases and deferred tax)n/an/an/an/a37.1%35.4%

Between FY20 and FY25 our simple ROCE rose from 16.6% to 38.2%. Revenue more than doubled (+119%) while capital employed grew only 25%. Asset turns (0.86x to 1.51x) explain about two-thirds of the improvement. EBIT margin (19.3% to 25.3%) explains about one-third. In FY26 asset turns fell back to 1.17x as acquisition capital entered the base with only 50 days of earnings. Every measure we can reproduce is lower in FY26 than FY25.

Why did ROCE rise from 17% to the high 30s? Not because the business suddenly became capital-light. It already was. The FY19 and FY20 base was depressed by a large cash and investment balance that earned little. Investments were 64% of assets in FY18, 39% in FY19 and 19% in FY20. EBITDA margin was also only 24%. Then the company shrank its capital base through payouts. Net worth went from ₹472 Cr in FY19 to ₹367 Cr in FY20 and FY22, even as revenue rose from ₹332 Cr to ₹449 Cr. Margin recovered, and revenue per rupee of capital rose from 0.86x to 1.51x [76]. FY15’s 49% ROCE sat on a balance sheet that was 56% financial investments, with a 36% EBITDA margin on a ₹224 Cr revenue base. The definitions differ (our measure of the same year is 29.7%). A ROCE earned when more than half the capital is cash is not comparable with today’s. The FY26 picture needs honesty about three versions. The aggregator says 39% (FY25: 41%). The audited ratio note says 35.40% on average capital employed including leases and deferred tax (FY25: 37.09%). The CEO’s letter says 38.2%, ‘sustained even after absorbing the full balance-sheet impact of the Unbound Medicine acquisition’ [1]. We could not reproduce 38.2% from the audited note’s own definition. On the audited basis, ROCE fell about 1.7 points. That is a modest dilution and exactly what an acquisition carried at 125% of its price in goodwill plus intangibles should cause in its first partial year. Trailing-twelve-month EBIT of ₹246 Cr over September 2026 capital of ₹721 Cr gives 34% on our simple basis [76].

FY19 to FY25651.265998.8%Returned over ₹650 Cr in 7 years per the annual report

MPS returned ₹651.2 Cr over FY19 to FY25. That is 98.8% of the ₹659 Cr of cumulative net profit and 99.7% of cumulative operating cash flow. It included a buyback (₹104.6 Cr in FY22) alongside dividends. Distribution has been lumpy by design: 31% of profit in FY23, 179% in FY22. Dividends declared for FY26 were nil. Cash paid out in FY26 was ₹86.7 Cr, being the FY25 final of ₹50 per share.

Dividend policy is the clearest example of an aggregator row hiding the story. The Screener payout of 0% in FY26 and 95% to 108% in FY24 and FY25 is correct on a declared basis. But the cash flow statement shows dividends paid of ₹134.5 Cr in FY25 (90% of that year’s profit) and ₹86.7 Cr in FY26 (50%). The FY25 final of ₹50 a share was paid in September 2025 [1], [16]. The promoter holding company took ₹58.5 Cr of the FY26 outflow (67%) [1]. For FY26 itself, the Directors’ Report states: “the Board of Directors of the Company has not recommended any dividend for the financial year 2025-26”. The CEO’s letter says “We did not pay a final dividend” because “the opportunity was larger than the cash in hand” [1]. Management restated the rule on the Q2 FY27 call: “On capital allocation, our first call this year is growth. And that includes acquisitions... Once that capital is deployed, distributions resume. The principle has held since FY19: capital must earn its return within 12 months, or it goes back to the shareholders.” (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q2 FY27) [2]. We found no dividend declaration for FY27 as of 13 November 2026. Our judgement is that the policy is real, and the ₹651 Cr record proves it was followed from FY19 to FY25. But it is a ‘residual’ policy, so dividends are a function of acquisition appetite and will stay lumpy. We do not model them as a steady stream.

The promoter holding company, ADI BPO Services, has held exactly 68.34% in all twelve quarters. Foreign institutions fell from 3.71% to 1.49%, domestic institutions rose from 0.56% to 2.11%, the public holding went from 26.53% to 27.27%, and the number of shareholders rose from 15,737 to 26,333 (peaking at 29,852 in September 2025). The free float is about 31.7%.

Dilution is not a concern. The share count has been 17,105,816 since the FY22 buyback. FY21 to FY22 profit rose 47.5% while EPS rose 57.0% because shares fell 6%. The audited reconciliation shows no issue or buyback in FY25 or FY26 [1], [76]. Employee options are small and are met mainly from shares the MPS Employee Welfare Trust bought in the market. There are 158,316 options outstanding under the 2023 scheme (0.93% of shares) and 139,884 shares in the trust (0.82%). A fifth grant of 79,009 options was approved on 4 May 2026 (0.46%). Share-based pay expense was ₹1.04 Cr in FY26, 0.14% of revenue [1]. Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora’s remuneration rose 47% to ₹6.86 Cr (0.9% of revenue, 4.0% of profit). That is notable but not large. Related-party business is also small. Rent and infrastructure charges to the promoter and a KMP-controlled entity totalled ₹3.1 Cr (0.4% of revenue). The security deposit with ADI BPO fell from ₹1.0 Cr to ₹0.83 Cr [1]. The most important governance item is not in the numbers. In July 2025 the Board approved a scheme to merge the promoter’s holding company into MPS, after demerger of its infrastructure-management and investing business. Shareholders approved it on 22 August 2026. The promoter group’s 11,690,615 shares and all institutional votes were in favour, as were 703,540 of the 703,590 votes cast by other public shareholders. The tribunal passed its second-motion order on 16 September and a hearing was listed for 4 November. We have not found the outcome of that hearing [1], [82], [83]. Per reports, the 68.34% block would be cancelled and an equal number of shares issued to ADI BPO’s shareholders. The share count and public shareholders’ percentages should not change [82]. We have not read the independent expert’s report, the fairness opinion or ADI BPO’s own financial statements. The liabilities MPS inherits remain unverified.

The auditor’s opinion has been unmodified in all three years, with no emphasis-of-matter paragraph. But every year’s key audit matters include acquisition accounting, and FY26 adds goodwill impairment. The same audit-trail exception has appeared for three consecutive years. The CARO schedule lists ₹36.3 Cr of disputed income-tax demands while the contingent-liability note shows ₹4.0 Cr. We could not reconcile the difference from the text.

A mentor’s reading of the audit pages is deliberately blunt. The positive side first. The audit opinion is clean and internal financial controls were reported effective. There were no going-concern doubts and no fraud reported in the CARO statement. There were no delays with statutory dues beyond ‘slight delays in a few cases’, and no covenant breach on the loan [1]. The negatives are pointed, not alarming. First, the audit-trail point. The proviso to Rule 3(1) of the Companies (Accounts) Rules requires accounting software with an edit log that cannot be disabled, for years beginning on or after 1 April 2023. Auditors report on five points under Rule 11(g) [84]. MPS’s auditor has reported the same exception in FY24, FY25 and FY26. The edit log is not enabled at database level in the main accounting software, and the third-party payroll provider’s assurance report is silent on it [15], [16], [1]. That is a systems fix, not an earnings issue. But three years without the fix is worth a line in the control-environment score. Second, the key audit matters. In each of three years the auditor has spent its most significant effort on purchase-price allocation, a subjective exercise in future cash flows, growth rates and discount rates. In FY26 it added the goodwill impairment test and noted a ₹12.9 Cr charge [1]. Third, the tax disputes. The CARO schedule lists income-tax demands totalling ₹36.3 Cr. Of these, ₹21.8 Cr relates to assessment year 2023-24 and sits before the Assessing Officer. The note on contingent liabilities shows only ₹4.0 Cr. The likeliest explanation is that most of the demand is already provided for or expected to be reversed, but the annual report does not say so [1].

Quality of exceptional itemsMixed₹29.7 Cr gains vs ₹22.0 Cr charges; ₹14.0 Cr AJE advances write-back on a policy changeB-
Acquisition accountingJudgement-heavyGoodwill 40% of assets; AJE goodwill cut 28% in measurement period; Unbound PPA provisional to Feb 2027C+
Goodwill impairment headroomAdequate but thin in one unitCorporate Learning goodwill 8.9x segment result; ₹12.9 Cr Liberate impairment takenB-
Tax rate and profit growthSmall flatteringTax 24.5% vs 26.0%; at last year’s rate FY26 profit would be ₹169.8 Cr (growth 14.0%, not 16.3%)B+

Currency exposureStructural, managed99.5% export revenue; hedges about 65% of net dollar balance-sheet exposure; planning at a conservative rateB
Aggregator vs audited cross-checkAgrees on core linesRevenue, EBITDA, PAT and CFO agree; other income, borrowings, EPS and payout differ in definition onlyA-

Ten of the sixteen tests grade A- or better, or B+. The weaker six are acquisition accounting and goodwill headroom, the quality of other income and the exceptional layer, control-environment hygiene, and structural currency exposure. Our overall grade for earnings quality is B+. The cash is strong and the balance sheet clean, but a non-trivial layer of accounting is subjective.

Five accounting and cash-flow things that could be wrong
#What could be wrongEvidence in the recordWhat to watch
1Unbound’s purchase-price allocation and goodwill move, or retention disappoints₹123.9 Cr goodwill (88% of price) plus ₹52.3 Cr intangibles; provisional until Feb 2027; AJE goodwill was cut 28% in the same exerciseRestated Unbound goodwill in the FY27 report; renewal rates and subscription revenue; the amortisation charge on the ₹52.3 Cr of intangibles
2Corporate Learning needs a larger impairmentGoodwill ₹100.4 Cr against segment result of ₹11.3 Cr; discount rates 14.5% to 19%Segment result margin (11.8% in FY26) holding above the first-half FY27 level; any second impairment
3FY26 profit overstated by non-repeatable accounting gains₹29.7 Cr gross exceptional gains; ₹14.0 Cr customer-advance write-back; ₹6.4 Cr ordinary write-backsExceptional gains above 5% of pre-tax profit; write-backs above 25% of other income
4Acquired deferred revenue turns into revenue without new cash, or working-capital drains recurUnbound brought ₹35.0 Cr of contract liabilities; FY25’s ₹64.4 Cr working-capital drain followed an acquisition-heavy FY24 and is not itemisedContract liabilities excluding acquisition and currency (fell ₹17.1 Cr in FY26, including the ₹14.0 Cr write-back); CFO/EBITDA below 70% without an explanation
5Tax disputes and control gaps are larger than disclosed₹36.3 Cr of disputed income-tax demands against ₹4.0 Cr in the note; audit-trail exception for three yearsOutcome of the AY2023-24 and AY2024-25 appeals; removal of the audit-trail exception; liabilities inherited from the promoter holdco merger

None of these is a present misstatement. Each is a place where the audited numbers rest on a judgement or an accounting reversal. A bad outcome would show up first as a write-down or a cash-conversion miss, not as an operating miss.

What would change our view on earnings quality (business-quality triggers only). First, CFO below 70% of EBITDA for two consecutive years with no acquisition explanation. Second, a second goodwill impairment in Corporate Learning, or an impairment in Research Solutions. Third, net exceptional gains above 5% of pre-tax profit again. Fourth, bank debt above 0.5 times EBITDA for more than two quarters. Fifth, a restated Unbound allocation that cuts goodwill sharply or raises it materially. Sixth, removal of the audit-trail exception, which would be a favourable change. Seventh, a merger scheme that brings unexpected liabilities from the promoter holding company.

Our independent judgement. On the audited record this is a high-quality earnings stream with a layer of subjective accounting on top. The strong parts are verified. There are ten years of 1.00x cash conversion and capital expenditure near 1% of revenue. FY26 free cash flow after leases was ₹183.6 Cr. The share count has not changed since FY22. The loan was repaid within six months, and ₹163 Cr of cash sat against zero bank debt at September 2026. Receivable days fell to 38. The audit opinion is clean with effective internal financial controls [1], [2], [76]. The weak parts are also verified. Other income is lumpy and 41% write-backs. The exceptional layer helped FY26 by ₹7.6 Cr net (₹29.7 Cr gross). A policy change released ₹14.0 Cr of AJE customer advances. The tax rate was 1.5 points lower than FY25. Goodwill and acquired intangibles are 52.5% of assets, and the Corporate Learning unit carries 8.9 times segment result in goodwill. Three versions of ROCE are in circulation, and the same audit-trail exception has appeared for three years [1], [16], [15]. Net, reported FY26 profit of ₹173.2 Cr includes about ₹3.5 Cr from a lower tax rate and ₹7.6 Cr (pre-tax) from exceptional items. Strip the exceptional items, and separately the write-backs and fair-value gains in other income. Underlying pre-tax growth was then between 12.6% and 13.5%, not the 16.3% reported. That is still a good number. We rate earnings quality B+. On the evidence, the cash-flow statement is more reliable than the P&L for this company.

Chapter 7

Chapters 1 to 6 judged the business without looking at a quote. This chapter puts a price on it. The verified ledger close is ₹2,297.00 (NSE, 12 November 2026), and all valuation figures here are struck at that price. A later TradingView pull, taken after the 13 November close, shows ₹2,259.30 (−1.64% on the day). It is not yet ledger-verified, so I call it the 13 Nov print and use it only where it changes an answer. The two prices are never blended. The share count is 17,105,816 (1.7106 Cr) and has not changed since the FY22 buyback [1]. Market capitalisation is therefore ₹3,929.2 Cr at the ledger close and ₹3,865 Cr at the print. Management-defined cash was ₹163 Cr at 30 September 2026. There is no bank debt, because the Unbound term loan was repaid in August [2]. Add about ₹18 Cr of lease liabilities and enterprise value is roughly ₹3,784 Cr.

The 13 Nov print₹2,259.30Market cap ₹3,865 Cr; EV ≈ ₹3,720 Cr22.3x FY26 EPS, 18.1x FY27E EPS, 11.8x FY27E EBITDA. Cheaper by about 1.6%, not by a regime.

At ₹2,297.00 the stock costs about 21 times trailing earnings, 18 times our FY27 estimate and 12 times forward EBITDA. The free-cash-flow yield is 5-6% and the return on capital is 35-39%. That is the profile of a good business at a fair price. It is not a distressed bargain and it is not a bubble. The table cannot show one thing. The FY27 EBITDA estimate is already about 33% above FY26, so recent growth is in the ratios. What remains to be earned is the next leg, FY28.

The first honest conclusion is modest. MPS is not cheap enough to buy on valuation alone. It is not so expensive that valuation is the reason to stay away. Everything depends on whether the earnings base can keep rising while an AI-deflation debate presses on the multiple. The next sections test that in three steps: where the multiple has been, what the price already assumes, and how peers are priced.

Today's 22.7x on FY26 EPS is exactly the five-year median. It sits near the 75th percentile of the full ten-year band. The multiple is therefore not depressed against recent history, and it is above the ten-year median of 17.6x. The stock is mid-pack against the last five years and rich-ish against the decade. It is a bargain on neither. The desk computed the band from the ledger price history and the aggregator's financial-year EPS. Each year's EPS is applied from 1 June after the fiscal year-end.

Three drawdowns of 50% or more since 2015 (−57.7%, −67.5%, −51.9%) are the base rate for this stock. The latest one happened while earnings grew. Between March 2025 and March 2026, trailing EPS rose from ₹69.43 to ₹87.05. Yet the multiple fell from 41.7x to 16.0x, a 61.6% compression. That accounts for more than the whole price fall. The multiple, not the earnings, is the volatile variable in this stock.

Why was 41.7x so high in March 2025? The market was capitalising an earnings-acceleration phase. EPS grew from ₹63.83 in FY23 to ₹69.43 in FY24 (+8.8%). It then jumped to ₹87.05 in FY25 (+25.4%) and ₹101.26 in FY26 (+16.3%) [76]. FY25 revenue grew 33%, and most of that came from the AJE acquisition. FY26 revenue growth was back to 6% [1]. A multiple measured on FY24 EPS looked absurd at 41.7x. Forward earnings were about to catch up, so the buyer was paying for FY25, not FY24. The 2023-25 multiple was inflated by that acceleration. It was also inflated by a market that extrapolated acquisition-fuelled growth. Once EPS growth settles in the mid-teens, a multiple between 17x and 23x is the sensible neighbourhood. That range runs from the ten-year median to the five-year median.

Why did the multiple then fall to 16.0x by March 2026? That multiple sat near the ten-year 25th percentile of 13.8x and above the November 2019 level of 14.4x. The honest answer is that it was not about MPS alone. In February and March 2026 an AI-disintermediation debate swept almost every labour-based services and information name. The dated record follows. Two of the sources are broker and aggregator blogs whose figures wobble between headline and body. I use them for date and order of magnitude, not for decimals.

13 Nov 2026 pullListed peers in the same debate, one year laterDatamatics −17.8%, NIIT Learning Systems −49.5%, Newgen Software −55.7%; all three in Stage 4 under the snapshot classifier; MPS −0.65%TradingView snapshot, 13 Nov

MPS touched its 2026 low in the very week the Indian IT index made its own. The de-rating from 41.7x to 16.0x was a sector repricing, not a company-specific discovery. That is the good news. The uncomfortable half is the next line. The peers have not recovered, and MPS did. Its one-year change of roughly −0.6% is a round trip (−51.9%, then +110.5%) rather than a stable plateau.

NARRATOR'S NOTE: a multiple is a mood about the terminal value

When earnings rise 25% and the multiple falls 60%, the market is not disagreeing about next year. It is changing its mind about the years after that. The 2026 AI debate is a debate about terminal value. Will content-production vendors still keep a margin in 2032? Nobody can answer that with a forecast, which is why the multiple swings so far.

The disciplined response is neither to buy the fear nor to wait for certainty. Work out what the price assumes about the years after next. Then ask whether our evidence is better or worse than that assumption. The reverse DCF that follows does exactly that.

Now the reverse DCF. Instead of forecasting and asking what the stock is worth, I take the price. I hold the discount rate and terminal growth fixed. Then I solve for the free-cash-flow growth that the price requires. The starting point is FY27E free cash flow to the firm of ₹214 Cr. It is built from EBITDA of ₹315 Cr, less 25% tax on EBIT after ₹36 Cr of depreciation, less ₹11 Cr of capex, ₹5 Cr of lease payments and a ₹15 Cr working-capital build. That is a 5.7% yield on EV. Growth then fades in a straight line over ten years, from the solved year-one rate to the terminal rate. I chose a fade because no business sustains 15% growth and then drops to 5% in year eleven. The model is the desk's own, in valuation.py. The grid extends it around the four central cases.

The number:

At ₹2,297.00 the market pays for free cash flow that starts at ₹214 Cr in FY27. If you demand a 13% return, the average annual growth along the fade path is 8.8% over ten years, with 12.6% in year one fading to 5%. The constant-rate equivalent is 9.2% a year.

How it was calculated:

This is a reverse discounted cash flow. Enterprise value of ₹3,784 Cr is set equal to ten years of free cash flow to the firm plus a terminal value. We solve for the year-one growth rate. Growth fades in a straight line to the terminal rate by year ten.

The inputs:

FY27E free cash flow to the firm of ₹214 Cr. It uses EBITDA ₹315 Cr, D&A ₹36 Cr, tax 25% on EBIT, capex ₹11 Cr, leases ₹5 Cr and a working-capital build of ₹15 Cr. The discount rate is 13% and terminal growth is 5%. Free cash flow reaches about ₹496 Cr by year ten.

The range:

At 12% and 5% terminal growth the price needs 9.1% in year one (about 7.0% a year on average). At 14% and 5% it needs 15.9% (about 10.4%). At 13% and 4% it needs 15.0% (about 9.5%). Across the full 11-15% by 3-6% grid the answer runs from 1.7% to 22.9%. The discount rate matters more than any other single assumption.

If the number is off:

If your hurdle is 14% rather than 13%, the price needs about 3 more points of year-one growth. That is hard against the FY15-26 EBITDA CAGR of 10.2%. It is easy against the FY21-26 CAGR of 17.1%. If free cash flow grows only 8% a year for ten years, a buyer at ₹2,297 earns about 12.4% a year.

Reverse DCF grid: implied year-one growth in free cash flow (%), fading linearly to terminal growth over ten years, at EV ₹3,784 Cr and FY27E FCFF ₹214 Cr
Discount rateTerminal growth 3%Terminal growth 4%Terminal growth 5%Terminal growth 6%
11%11.08.35.21.7
12%14.211.89.16.0
13%17.215.012.69.9
14%20.118.115.913.4
15%22.921.018.916.7

Read the 12-14% rows and the 4-5% columns. They bracket a sensible Indian small-cap hurdle and the long-run growth of a USD-earning business. The market needs year-one growth of roughly 9% to 18% in that zone. The 13% and 5% cell (12.6%) is the central case. The harsh corner is 3% terminal growth with a 15% hurdle (22.9%). That is where the AI bears effectively live. They assume the franchise has little growth after year ten and demand a high return for the risk.

What a buyer at ₹2,297.00 earns if free cash flow grows at a constant rate for ten years, then 5% (EV ₹3,784 Cr; start ₹214 Cr)
Ten-year FCF growthCompared withImplied annual return
5.0%Terminal rate only10.9%
8.0%Below any recorded EBITDA CAGR12.4%
10.2%FY15-26 EBITDA CAGR13.5%
12.7%FY21-26 sales CAGR (FY15-26: 11.9%)14.9%
17.1%FY21-26 EBITDA CAGR17.7%

If MPS merely repeats its longest-run EBITDA growth of 10.2%, the buyer earns about 13.5% a year. If it repeats the last five years (17.1%), the return is about 17.7%. A constant 9.2% for ten years is what a 13% return needs. It is 7.2% at a 12% return and 11.1% at 14%. The price therefore assumes a decent decade. That is below what the company delivered in the last five years and roughly equal to what it delivered over eleven.

Now put that against delivered history and guidance. EBITDA compounded at 17.1% from FY21 to FY26 and at 10.2% from FY15 to FY26. Sales compounded at 12.7% and 11.9% over the same windows [76]. Both EBITDA windows include acquisitions, and AJE in FY25 was the main one. The FY21 base was also depressed by the pandemic and a 37% tax rate, so the 17.1% flatters. The FY27 guide is a floor of ₹310 Cr, raised from ₹300 Cr on 4 November. H1 FY27 EBITDA was ₹154 Cr. The CFO said: “We are now raising the floor, and we expect FY27 EBITDA to be at least INR310 crores.” (CFO Prarthana Agarwal, Q2 FY27) [2] That is +31% on FY26's ₹236 Cr, and our desk figure of ₹315 Cr is +33%. Our FY27E free cash flow already contains it. The Vision-2027 destination is ₹450 Cr of EBITDA and ₹1,500 Cr of revenue in FY28. The CEO told the Q1 FY27 call that this “probably will translate to more like INR450 crores EBITDA.” (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q1 FY27) [25] That is 43% above our FY27E. The CEO himself says the revenue target combines organic growth with acquisitions. Our base case of ₹360 Cr is 14% above FY27E and needs no deal.

Read together, the price assumes something close to the middle of delivered history, not the top. It does not pay for Vision-2027. At ₹360 Cr of base FY28E EBITDA the forward P/E is 16.0x on FY28E EPS of ₹143.4. That is a forward multiple, so it is not comparable with the trailing band. On base LTM EPS at the value date (₹133.9) the multiple is 17.2x. On FY27E EPS of ₹124.5, which is the band's own basis, it is 18.4x. A figure of ₹450 Cr would be a large upside surprise. That is the case for the stock. The case against is where the market's scepticism sits. It explains why a business this good is not priced higher. (1) AI price give-back. The CEO's own words are that “Customers expect to share in our productivity, and they do, but we kind of make that up through more volume” (Chairman and CEO Rahul Arora, Q2 FY27) [2]. A 20% unit-price cut needs 25% more volume to hold revenue. The evidence that MPS keeps the savings is essentially one segment's margin trend. (2) Research margin durability. Research is about 60% of revenue and about 65% of segment EBITDA. Its margin climbed from roughly 37-42% a year ago to 48.2% in Q2 FY27 [2]. On FY26 Research revenue of ₹463.5 Cr, every point of margin is about ₹4.6 Cr of EBITDA. A return to 40% would remove about ₹37 Cr, which is the arithmetic of our bear case [1]. (3) Concentration. The largest customer was 17.3% of FY26 revenue and the top 15 were 58% [1]. (4) Acquisition accounting. Goodwill plus acquired intangibles are 52.5% of total assets. The Unbound purchase-price allocation is provisional until February 2027. Corporate Learning carries ₹100.4 Cr of goodwill against a segment result of ₹11.3 Cr [1]. None of the four is a flaw in the audited record. Each is a reason the market refuses to pay 29x again.

Now the peers. Three listed Indian companies share enough DNA with MPS to deserve a table. Datamatics sells publishing and data services through Lumina Datamatics and TNQTech. NIIT Learning Systems runs managed training services. Newgen Software builds content and process platforms. None is a clean comparable. All three are in Stage 4 under the snapshot classifier. The table is rendered from the 13 November TradingView pull.

MPS has the highest margin in the set, at a 29.45% TTM operating margin against 12.9% to 23.7%. It also has the highest ROCE and the least leverage. Its P/E of 20.74 is the highest in the set, against Datamatics at 18.87, Newgen at 19.6 and NIIT Learning Systems at 9.87. MPS trades about 10% above Datamatics and about 6% above Newgen. In return it gets almost twice the operating margin and almost twice the ROCE of Datamatics (38.7% against 20.4%). The one-year column is the real signal. The three peers are down 18-56% while MPS is roughly flat. The AI de-rating is still open for them. For MPS it is largely done, and then reversed. That is a reason to respect the market's verdict, and a warning that MPS has already had its relief rally. Either MPS's platform, journal and author-services mix earns the gap or it is the first thing a price-aggressive rival attacks. This is the margin the bears target.

Two firms in the table show deflation or shrinkage. They are RWS and Skillsoft. Two show that AI-era demand rewards proprietary content or scarce human judgment. They are Wolters Kluwer Health and Innodata. The Indian peer shows the margin gap. MPS spans all of these, so it is neither a clean AI winner nor a clean loser. It owns journals, platforms and a medical-knowledge asset that behave like Wolters Kluwer's. It also has a large production book that behaves like RWS's. The valuation question is how the profit pool is split between the two over the next five years.

Now to the scenarios. We value the stock twelve months ahead, around November 2027. A trailing multiple must be applied to trailing earnings, so the valuation shows two readings. The ten-year band is built on last-reported fiscal-year EPS. At a November 2027 value date, that basis means FY27E EPS. We call it basis A, and it is the stress case. Markets price on current earnings while the band lags, so we also show basis B. It uses last-twelve-months (LTM) EPS at the value date, the average of FY27E and FY28E EPS. Basis B is the more generous reading and our central case. The first table uses basis B. The two-basis table after it shows both. All cases assume D&A of ₹36 Cr in FY27E and ₹38 Cr in FY28E, other income of ₹8 Cr, interest of ₹3 Cr, a 25% tax rate and 1.7106 Cr shares. Only EBITDA and the multiple change. The multiples are anchored on the band measured above, not invented. Bear 14x is near the 25th percentile (13.8x). Base 18x is near the ten-year median (17.6x). Bull 22x is near the five-year median (22.7x). The bull case needs no multiple expansion beyond where the stock trades today.

On basis B the probability-weighted value of ₹2,440 is only 6.2% above the ledger close. On valuation alone the stock is close to fairly priced. The spread runs from −29.1% to +44.1%. The upside-to-downside ratio of the extreme cases is 1.5 to 1, and the base case offers just +4.9%. A 29% loss is what we would live with if the AI-deflation argument is right. This is an adequate business at an adequate price. It is not a margin of safety. The picture improves with a lower entry. The same weights give +13.5% from ₹2,150 and +17.9% from ₹2,070. The base value of ₹2,410 is 19.4x FY27E EPS of ₹124.5, between the ten-year median and the 75th percentile.

Basis A is the band's own basis, so it is the stricter test. On basis A the base value is ₹2,241, which is 2.4% below the ledger close. The weighted value is ₹2,254, which is 1.9% below it. Basis B gives ₹2,440. We treat B as central because markets price on current earnings, while the band is built on a lagging EPS. Basis A therefore understates what the market typically pays. We keep it as the stress case and size the position so the verdict survives it. The 25/50/25 weights are also a judgment. Moving to 35/45/20 would lower the basis B weighted value to about ₹2,317. The verdict does not depend on the weights. The decision rule is reward-to-risk at the support zone.

The ₹2,070 level clears the hurdle on both bases, so it is the core entry. The ₹2,150 level clears it only on basis B. It needs a base value of at least ₹2,367.50. Basis B clears that line by 1.8%. Basis A misses it by ₹126.5. On basis A the line needs FY27E EPS of about ₹131.5, which is FY27 EBITDA of roughly ₹331 Cr. That is why the top of the zone is conditional and the bottom is not.

Each scenario ties a business outcome to a multiple already seen in the stock's own history. The bear and bull cases are not extremes of the band. They sit at its 25th percentile and its five-year median. That keeps the exercise honest about how far the multiple can move without a new regime.
