Even the bear case has LTM EPS 15.0% above FY26's ₹101.26, because H1 FY27 EBITDA of ₹154 Cr is already banked. The bear case is a valuation story, not an earnings collapse. It needs FY27 to land on ₹300 Cr and FY28 EBITDA to fall 6% from the base FY27E. EBITDA last fell in FY20, to ₹79 Cr from ₹94 Cr.

The number:

Base-case LTM EPS (basis B) at the value date, about November 2027, is ₹133.9. It is the average of FY27E EPS of ₹124.5 and FY28E EPS of ₹143.4. At 18x that gives ₹2,410 per share.

How it was calculated:

Compute EPS for each year. FY27E EBITDA of ₹315 Cr less D&A of ₹36 Cr, plus other income of ₹8 Cr, less interest of ₹3 Cr gives ₹284 Cr before tax. Tax at 25% leaves ₹213.0 Cr, or ₹124.5 per share. FY28E EBITDA of ₹360 Cr less D&A of ₹38 Cr, plus ₹8 Cr, less ₹3 Cr gives ₹327 Cr before tax and ₹245.3 Cr after tax, or ₹143.4 per share. Average the two years. Shares are 1.7106 Cr.

The inputs:

Base FY27 EBITDA is ₹315 Cr, above the guided floor of at least ₹310 Cr. Base FY28 EBITDA is ₹360 Cr, about 14% higher. Bear uses ₹300 Cr and ₹295 Cr. Bull uses ₹330 Cr and ₹420 Cr. Tax is 25%, because the H1 FY27 rate reverted from about 20% to 25%. The share count is unchanged. Management says the ADI BPO merger cancels and reissues the same number of shares.

The range:

Bear LTM EPS is ₹116.4 (FY27E ₹117.9, FY28E ₹114.9). Bull LTM EPS is ₹150.4 (FY27E ₹131.1, FY28E ₹169.7). If both years' EBITDA moved by ₹10 Cr, LTM EPS would move about ₹4.4. At 18x that is about ₹79 per share, or 3.4% of today's price. Each turn of the multiple on base LTM EPS is about ₹134 per share, or 5.8% of today's price.

If the number is off:

The multiple matters more than EBITDA here. A one-turn miss costs about as much as a ₹17 Cr miss in both years' EBITDA. The ₹2,150 tranche clears our 1.5-to-1 hurdle only while the base value stays at or above ₹2,367.50. On basis B the cushion is just 1.8%, equal to FY28E EBITDA of about ₹350 Cr. On basis A, the base value of ₹2,241 misses the line by ₹126.5. If Research margin holds near 48% and acquisitions close, the bull case applies. If H2 FY27 EBITDA lands below ₹156 Cr, the floor is at risk and we move weight to the bear case.

Historical Cycle & Valuation Twin: Wolters Kluwer (information, software and services)

Netherlands, global; the AI-disruption de-rating of February to November 2026

Shared Mechanism: A quality, cash-generative information franchise with 83% recurring revenue was sold off on a technology-disruption narrative [65]. On 3 February 2026 Anthropic released a legal plug-in for Claude. Thomson Reuters, RELX and Wolters Kluwer fell 16%, 14% and 13% in a day. Morningstar noted that the stocks had been de-rating since summer 2025. It kept its wide-moat view that day. On 5 March 2026 it downgraded Wolters Kluwer and Thomson Reuters to a narrow moat [85]. The reported fundamentals did not move.

Historical Numbers & Outcome: FY25 revenue was €6,125m, up 6% organically. Adjusted operating margin was 27.5% (27.1% in 2024). Diluted adjusted EPS was €5.29, up 9% in constant currencies. Adjusted free cash flow was €1,348m [65]. In the first nine months of 2026, organic growth was 6% and adjusted operating profit rose 10% in constant currency. Guidance was reaffirmed on 4 November 2026 [89]. The company's own buyback prices show the multiple. The average was €128.45 in 2025 (8.6m shares, €1.1bn). It was €75.79 from January to 24 February 2026. It was €67.97 to 3 November 2026 (3.9m shares, €264m) [65], [89]. On FY25 EPS of €5.29 that is about 24.3x, 14.3x and 12.8x (desk arithmetic). The average price fell about 47% while EPS rose. Average buyback prices are a proxy for the trading price, not a quote.

Direct Implication for MPSLTD: A good franchise de-rated 47% while its earnings kept rising. Either the market is right that the terminal value is impaired, or it is paying you to wait. Wolters Kluwer shows that the answer comes from organic growth and margin over the next several reporting periods, not from the multiple. MPS followed the same arc, with 41.7x falling to 16.0x while EPS rose. The twin also cuts the other way. Morningstar's narrow-moat downgrade means the de-rating was not irrational. Management puts MPS's recurring revenue at about 45% [25] against Wolters Kluwer's 83%. The case for a permanent discount is therefore stronger here.

Valuation trigger. We raise fair value only if three business metrics hold. Then the base multiple moves from 18x towards 20x, or FY28E EBITDA rises above ₹360 Cr. The first metric is H2 FY27 EBITDA of at least ₹156 Cr (the ₹310 Cr floor less H1's ₹154 Cr). The second is a Research Solutions margin of at least 45% for two further quarters. The third is a largest customer at or below 17.3% of revenue. An acquisition near 2x revenue, funded from the ₹163 Cr of cash, would also lift the bull weighting if it returns more than 25% within 12 months. We cut fair value toward the bear case on any of five events. Research margin falls below 42% for two quarters. FY27 EBITDA guidance is cut below ₹300 Cr. Corporate Learning margin slips below 18% for two quarters. A goodwill impairment above ₹25 Cr is booked. The ADI BPO scheme terms change to be dilutive to public holders.

Now to the bridge to Chapter 8. On every valuation measure above, the price is not demanding. The reverse DCF needs only about 7% to 10% a year of free-cash-flow growth over ten years. The stock trades at the five-year median multiple. The probability-weighted value is ₹2,440 on basis B and ₹2,254 on the stricter basis A. So why does the tape say wait? Because valuation does not by itself justify buying. Asymmetry is the test. The invalidation is a daily close below ₹2,005. At the ledger close of ₹2,297.00 the base-value ratio is (2,410 − 2,297) ÷ (2,297 − 2,005), or 0.39 to 1 on basis B. On basis A it is −0.19 to 1, because the base value of ₹2,241 sits below the price. The 13 Nov print of ₹2,259.30 gives 0.59 to 1 and −0.07 to 1. All of these fail our 1.5-to-1 hurdle. At the top of the support zone, ₹2,150, the ratio is 1.79 to 1 on basis B but only 0.63 to 1 on basis A. At ₹2,070 it is 5.23 to 1 on basis B and 2.63 to 1 on basis A. So the ₹2,070 level clears 1.5 to 1 on both bases, while ₹2,150 clears it only on the LTM basis. The weighted return from ₹2,150 is +13.5% on basis B and +4.8% on basis A. From ₹2,070 it is +17.9% and +8.9%. The business passes, and the price is fair. The entry point is what is missing. Chapter 8 explains why the tape offers it only lower. That tape is a Stage 3 reading in the ledger and Stage 4 in the 13 Nov snapshot. It shows a falling 50-day average and nine distribution days in 25 sessions.

Chapter 8

The first seven chapters judged the business. This one judges the tape, and the tape disagrees with the business on timing. On the verified 12 Nov 2026 NSE ledger, MPS closed at ₹2,297.00 (+1.48% on the day, -8.70% over a month). The ledger classifies it as Stage 3 (Topping / Pullback) [90]. The TradingView pull taken after the 13 Nov close shows ₹2,259.30 (-1.64%). Its classifier says Stage 4, entry posture AVOID_STAGE4_OR_BROKEN, timing gate CAP_REJECTED [91]. We date this report by the verified ledger, so ₹2,297.00 is the reference price. The 13 Nov figure is a later print that the ledger has not yet verified. We never merge the two. Our reading is that MPS sits exactly on the Stage 3 / Stage 4 boundary. The stricter of the two readings governs the timing gate.

Momentum screen and No-FOMO support evaluation

The question here is simple: is MPSLTD close enough to a rising support level to be bought without chasing? On the 12 November close it is not. Price sits 1.89 ATR under a falling 50-day average and about 11% above the 200-day. The support band and stop below show where that answer would change.

This evaluation separates MPSLTD's medium-term trend from entry timing, so a screen signal does not become an unsupported order.

The tape agrees with the valuation work in one respect and disagrees in another. The price is not demanding, but the trend has not turned. We therefore wait for support at the rising 200-day average instead of buying at ₹2,297.00 or at the 13 November print of ₹2,259.30.

A word on the method before the numbers. Stan Weinstein's stage analysis sorts every stock into four phases: base, advance, top and decline. It uses the 30-week moving average as the referee. Price above a rising 30-week line is strength. Price whipsawing around a flattening line is basing or topping. Price below a falling line is trouble [92]. The rule most useful to a quality-business investor is what Weinstein calls the oath. Do not buy into a Stage 4 decline, however good the story sounds. Wait for a Stage 1 base before even considering it [92]. Stage 3 differs from a Stage 1 base in what came before it, which is an advance and not a decline. It also brings heavy two-sided volume. Nothing on this page says MPS is a bad business. It says the stock is not yet in the phase where Weinstein would put new money to work. The price history in this chapter shows why that discipline matters for this stock.

On the 13 Nov print, four of the five averages sit above the price. Only the 200-day sits below. The 30-week line is being tested rather than defended. The 200-day is the next structural floor, and the entry zone is built on it. Ledger values are as of 12 Nov. The weekly values and the 13 Nov distances come from the TradingView pull [90], [91]. The compiled technical table strikes its extension figures on the 13 Nov print (₹2,259.30). They are -6.49% from the 10-week EMA and -9.68% from the 50-day. On the 12 Nov close (₹2,297.00) the same gaps are -4.9% and -8.2%, against the snapshot's 10-week EMA (₹2,416.11) and 50-day (₹2,501.56).

Put the four lines together. The stock has not yet broken its long-term trend, but it has lost its short-term one. That is why the ledger says Stage 3 and the snapshot says Stage 4. Both are defensible. The ledger works on daily averages. Price below a falling 50-day and above a rising 200-day is, by construction, the pullback-or-top zone [90]. The snapshot classifier works on the weekly shelf. A close below the 30-week SMA plus a weak trend means Stage 4 [91]. Our own recomputation from the daily history adds a detail. The weekly close of 6 Nov (₹2,232.90) was already marginally under the 30-week average (about ₹2,245). So two of the last three weekly closes sit below the line, each by a hair. The 30-week average itself has not yet turned down. That is the honest argument for calling this a boundary rather than a confirmed decline. We still take the stricter reading for the timing gate, because the cost of being wrong is asymmetric. If we wait on a quality stock and it rebounds from the zone, we lose a few per cent of upside. If we enter and the stock confirms Stage 4, we can lose the 20% to 30% that the shelf structure below it implies.

Momentum is soft on every frame but not washed out. RSI of 40.7 to 46.55 is neither oversold nor a base. The tool's auto-text calls the setup 'DISLOCATION_AT_MAJOR_SHELF'. It says price is 'stabilising above' the ₹2,262.01 shelf with 4H momentum 'turning up'. The print contradicts both phrases. It is 0.12% below the shelf, below the 4H 20-period EMA the text says it is above, and the tool's own 4H turn flag is false. Read it as price on the shelf. A weekly close further below the shelf would confirm Stage 4 [91].

The tool also generates an options idea, a call-spread window toward the 50-day average. We set that aside. MPS has no NSE futures-and-options contract, and the tool itself marks the stock NOT_OPTIONABLE_OR_ILLIQUID. Any view on this name can only be expressed in the cash equity. No derivative overlay exists to hedge or leverage the timing risk [91].

Heaviest session since the peak day4 Nov (results day): 65,881 shares; low ₹2,155.00, close ₹2,315.90 (-1.47%)The low was 8.3% under the prior close, then buyers absorbed it. With the 27 Oct low of ₹2,152.20, the zone just above ₹2,150 has now been tested twice.
Typical liquidity20-day average 18,184 shares (about ₹4.2 Cr); 63-day 18,790 (about ₹4.9 Cr)Thin. See the sizing note below.

Sellers have been heavier than buyers on average, but at tiny absolute size. Volume has collapsed to roughly a third of its rally-period level. This is distribution into a thin market, not capitulation. The only candle that looks like capitulation is the 4 Nov results day. The stock bounced from its low rather than closing on it [90].

Two features of that table deserve emphasis. First, the volume ratio is a mild warning. A 1.33x skew of down-day volume over up-day volume is what Weinstein would call churn: stock changing hands on both sides without progress [92]. It is nowhere near the 2x to 3x surge on rising prices that he demands before calling a breakout. Second, the 4 Nov session is the closest thing this stock has had to a flush. The company raised its FY27 EBITDA floor that day. Yet the shares traded 8.3% below the previous close intraday and then recovered. A guidance raise that the stock cannot hold is a classic late-distribution sign. We treat the quick recovery as evidence that the ₹2,150 area has buyers. It is not proof that the low is in.

Price map: where the March 2026 base, the August 2026 peak and the retracement levels sit (daily closes, computed with Python)

Mar 2026 base₹1,336.10 intraday low; ₹1,366.70 daily close low (9 Mar); ₹1,392.6 weekly close low-39.4% to -41.8%The structural floor of the whole rebuild

The Mar to Aug advance ran +114.5% on daily closes, from ₹1,366.70 on 9 Mar to ₹2,931.20 on 7 Aug. At the 12 Nov close the stock has given back 40.5% of that leg. At the 13 Nov print it has given back 42.9%. The 50% retracement lands at ₹2,148.95, almost exactly on the top of our entry zone. The 61.8% level at ₹1,964.34 sits below the stop. That is why a daily close below ₹2,005 would signal more than a normal pullback [90].

Now the liquidity point, because it matters more here than the chart. MPS has about 17.1 million shares in issue. The promoter group (ADI BPO Services) holds 68.34%, so the free float is roughly 31.7%. That is worth about ₹1,244 Cr at the ledger close [93]. The stock trades about 15,000 to 19,000 shares a day, or roughly ₹3.5 Cr to ₹4.5 Cr on recent averages. For a portfolio of this size that is not a binding constraint. The half-tier first fill of about 3.25% of NAV is roughly ₹1.39 lakh, or about 65 whole shares. That is well under 1% of one average day's value. Liquidity does matter for execution. The daily ATR is ₹116 (5.1% of price), the book is thin, and some sessions total only 6,719 shares. A single order can gap through a level and print a spike. We therefore recommend limit prices rather than market orders on any entry or exit. Work each tranche over several sessions rather than in one clip. The ₹2,005 stop is a daily close, not an intraday print. On 27 Oct the stock traded down to ₹2,152.20 and closed at ₹2,218.60. A stop that fires on an intraday spike in a thin tape is a donation to the market maker.

Who owns the free float: selected quarters from the shareholding pattern (promoter holding constant at 68.34%)
Quarter endFIIDIIPublicShareholders
Dec 20233.71%0.56%26.53%15,737

Mar 20261.47%1.96%27.41%25,010
Jun 20261.38%2.14%27.31%25,565
Sep 20261.49%2.11%27.27%26,333

Foreign institutions have sold about 60% of their stake since December 2023 (3.71% to 1.49%). Domestic institutions have more than tripled theirs from a tiny base (0.56% to 2.11%). The number of shareholders is up 67% to 26,333. The rise to ₹2,931 was therefore carried largely by retail and a few domestic funds, not by foreign money. That is a thin, price-sensitive buyer base [93].

What does that say about the marginal buyer? Combined FII and DII ownership went from 4.27% to 3.60% over the period. Institutions in aggregate are therefore net sellers of the free float since the end of 2023. Domestic funds did begin accumulating from the December 2025 quarter, a few months before the shares made their low [93]. The Sep 2026 quarter ended with the stock near ₹2,700. The next filing (Dec 2026) will show who bought and sold the 15% fall since. Two readings are possible. The bullish one is that low foreign ownership means little overhang and room for a re-rating once growth is recognised. The cautious one is that institutions own only 3.6% and 31.7% of the equity is in public hands. Each rally then depends on retail enthusiasm, and each decline meets thin bids. We do not need to choose. We only note that no heavy institutional sponsor is visible in the Weinstein sense. There is no obvious large buyer to defend a break of the shelf. ADI BPO's amalgamation into MPS is, on management's account, a cancellation and re-issue of the same number of shares. It leaves public holders undiluted. But the NCLT outcome from the 4 Nov hearing is unconfirmed. Until it lands, the free-float question is not fully closed.

Over a year MPS is flat (-0.65%), while Datamatics, NIIT Learning Systems and Newgen are down 18% to 56%. Over six months it is the only name in the set that is up (+38.33%). Over three months the gap has closed: -17.85% against -16.56%, -29.20% and -18.22%. Relative strength against the Nifty is -26.74% over 28 days and -22.76% over 63 days. MPS is the leader of a collapsing group that has itself started to roll over. TheWrap's weekly Stage 2 universe (465 stocks, as of 8 Nov 2026) contains none of the four [91], [94].

The market backdrop is no help and no hindrance. The ledger's regime reading is Uptrend (narrow breadth). 61.0% of the screened universe sits above its 50-day average, and the Nifty shows 4 distribution days [90]. TheWrap's list shows 465 Indian stocks in Stage 2 as of 8 Nov (328 continuing, 91 re-entries, 46 new). Only 28% of its IT Services group qualifies, and MPS is not among the 465 [94]. Many stocks have better technical credentials. That is the practical reason the opportunity cost of waiting is low.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: A relative-strength leader that has stopped leading

It is tempting to read the peer table as a compliment. MPS held its value while three competitors lost a fifth to a half of theirs. Surely the market is voting for the better business. I think that is half right. The market did separate MPS from its peers for most of the year. The ROCE, margin and debt numbers in chapters 4 and 6 explain why.

But relative strength measures what has happened. Over the last three months MPS has fallen as hard as the group it outran. A leader that stops leading is not a laggard. It is no longer the stock to chase. That is the point of Stage 3. The best names are the last to crack, and they crack from a higher price.

Price cycles since 2015 (weekly closes) matched to the business events from Pass 1 and the trailing-FY-EPS P/E at each turn
LegMove and lengthP/E (trailing FY)Business events behind it
Apr 2015 ₹1,022 to Feb 2019 ₹432-57.7% over 46 months31.0x to 11.5xFY15 QIP cash sat idle, and ROCE fell from 49% to 23%. FY18 sales fell 8%. Then the Tata Interactive acquisition (31 May 2018) took the operating margin from 33% to 26% in FY19.
Feb 2019 ₹432 to Nov 2019 ₹588+36.1% over about 8 months11.5x to 14.4xFY19 sales rose 36% from the consolidated acquisition. The business had become cheap relative to its cash generation, and the stock staged a relief rally.
Nov 2019 ₹588 to Mar 2020 ₹191-67.5% in under 5 months14.4x to 4.7xFY20 was the trough. Price cuts, vendor consolidation and a platform customer moving work to its captive took FY20 revenue to ₹334 Cr and cut EBITDA by ₹11 Cr. Then came the Covid shock in March 2020.
Mar 2020 ₹191 to Mar 2025 ₹2,898+1,416% over 59 months4.7x to 41.7xHighWire (Jul 2020) lifted platforms from 16% to 33% of revenue. Then came the eLearning restructuring, the FY22 buyback, EI Design, Liberate and AJE (Feb 2024). FY25 sales rose 33% and ROCE reached about 41%. Even in this run the stock fell about 33% in May 2022.
Mar 2025 ₹2,898 to Mar 2026 ₹1,393-51.9% over 11 months41.7x to 16.0xAJE revenue was cut by more than a quarter. FY26 sales rose 5.6% and Corporate Learning fell 16.5%. There was no final dividend. The Unbound deal came on 9 Feb 2026. Peers that have since fallen 18% to 56% over a year point to a sector-wide de-rating.
Mar 2026 ₹1,393 to Aug 2026 ₹2,931+110.5% over 5 months16.0x to 28.9xQ1 FY27 revenue was ₹224.24 Cr (+20.4%), EBITDA ₹76.96 Cr at 34.3%, and Research margin 45.1%. The stock re-rated from 16x to 29x on one quarter. The close high came on a 6.1% gain on 130,151 shares.
Aug 2026 ₹2,931 to 12 Nov ₹2,297-21.6% so far (14 weeks)28.9x to 22.7xQ2 FY27 beat and the FY27 EBITDA floor was raised to at least ₹310 Cr. The multiple compressed anyway. Price is down 20.7% from the Mar 2025 peak.

Three drawdowns of 50% or more (-57.7%, -67.5%, -51.9%) in eleven years, and two of the three began at a trailing P/E of 31x or higher. The business events line up with the turns. Each time, though, the price move was several times larger than the earnings change, because the multiple did most of the work. Today's 22.7x equals the five-year median. The full weekly band since Jun 2016 has a median of 17.6x, a 75th percentile of 23.0x and a maximum of 41.7x [90], [1].

The cycle table is the most important evidence in this chapter for how much to put on and how patient to be. The stock has gone through three drawdowns of 50% or more since 2015. Each came while the business was either executing a strategy change (Tata Interactive, the FY20 reset) or being re-rated by the market (2025 to 2026). The stock has also produced one of the strongest runs on the exchange, +1,416% in 59 months. Both are true of the same stock. This is a high-beta compounder in the literal sense. The compounding comes with drawdowns that a 6.5% tier cap cannot absorb without pain. That is why our rule for MPS is a first fill capped at half the tier cap, about 3.25% of NAV if both tranches fill. It is split into two unequal tranches. Two-thirds is the core order at ₹2,070. The smaller third at ₹2,150 is conditional. The balance waits for a confirmed base and a fresh re-underwrite. At the 12 Nov close the stock is already 21.6% off its high, so some of the reset has happened. But the Mar 2025 to Mar 2026 leg shows what the full version looks like. The trailing P/E went from 41.7x to 16.0x in 11 months.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: What three 50% drawdowns do to a position

If you had bought at the ₹1,022 peak in 2015, you would not have seen a weekly close above it again until February 2023. If you had bought at ₹588 in November 2019, you would have lost two-thirds of it in under five months. Yet someone who held through all of it from March 2020 would show a 26.1% annual return over five years (price only, to 12 Nov 2026). Both statements are true. They are the whole argument for patience with this stock.

I do not read the cycle table as a reason to avoid MPS. I read it as a reason to choose my own entry price instead of letting the market choose it for me. It is also a reason to size so that a 50% fall is an inconvenience rather than a crisis.

Here is the entry-zone logic, level by level. The zone is ₹2,070.00 to ₹2,150.00. Its upper edge is the 20-day swing low of ₹2,152.20. That level was tested on 27 Oct and again, within ₹3, on 4 Nov. It also coincides with the 50% retracement at ₹2,148.95. Its lower edge is the rising 200-day average at ₹2,069.06 and TradingView's monthly S1 at ₹2,065.93. The snapshot's own support band of ₹2,034.94 to ₹2,096.92 brackets the same area [91]. Four independent anchors cluster in a 4% band, which is what a real support area looks like. The stop is a daily close below ₹2,005.00. It sits below the 200-day by about half an ATR and below the snapshot's ₹2,010.85. It also lies above the 61.8% retracement at ₹1,964.34. The distance from ₹2,150 to ₹2,005 is 6.74% (1.25 ATR). That is tight enough to cap the loss and wide enough that normal noise should not trigger it. Do not confuse the stop with the Stage 4 signal. A weekly close below the 30-week average (₹2,262.01) is the Stage 4 confirmation. A daily close below ₹2,005.00 is the exit.

Now the reward-to-risk. We test the base value on two earnings bases. Our ten-year P/E band is built on the last reported fiscal-year EPS. At a Nov 2027 value date, that basis means FY27E EPS. The band's multiples (14x, 18x, 22x) then give a base value of ₹2,241. Call that basis A, the stress case. Valuing on last-twelve-months EPS at the value date gives a base value of ₹2,410, which is 18x EPS of ₹133.9. Call that basis B, our central case. Markets price on current earnings and the band uses a lagging EPS, so basis A understates what the market usually pays. Against the ₹2,005 stop, reward-to-risk at ₹2,070 is 2.63:1 on basis A and 5.23:1 on basis B. At ₹2,150 it is 0.63:1 on basis A and 1.79:1 on basis B. At the ledger close of ₹2,297.00 it is -0.19:1 and 0.39:1. At the 13 Nov print of ₹2,259.30 it is -0.07:1 and 0.59:1. The 1.5:1 hurdle is met on both bases only at ₹2,070. The ₹2,150 tranche clears it on basis B alone. It needs a base value of at least ₹2,367.50. Basis B clears that line by 1.8%, and basis A misses it by ₹126.50. The probability-weighted value is ₹2,254 on basis A and ₹2,440 on basis B. From ₹2,297.00 that is -1.9% and +6.2%. From ₹2,150 it is +4.8% and +13.5%. From ₹2,070 it is +8.9% and +17.9%. That is the whole reason for waiting. The business passes, and the price does not yet. The 25/50/25 weights are a judgment. Moving to 35/45/20 would lower the basis B weighted value to about ₹2,317. The decision does not depend on the weights, because the rule is the reward-to-risk at the support zone.

How can we hold the Stage 4 reading and still post limit prices? They are two different trades. The Stage 4 reading forbids buying at the current price or on a bounce. Weinstein's oath applies there [92]. The ₹2,070 order is the core of the plan. It sits at the rising 200-day average (₹2,069.06), which stood at ₹1,918.86 63 sessions ago. The long-term uptrend is intact at that level. The order clears 1.5:1 on both earnings bases (2.63:1 and 5.23:1). The ₹2,150 order is smaller and conditional. It is placed only if the re-derived base value is at least ₹2,367.50 on the day. Both are half-size, counter-trend support entries. We abandon them if the daily close breaks ₹2,005. Neither is a Stage 2 entry. Neither claims that the trend has turned.

The sizing follows from the two bases. The starter is about 3.25% of NAV (₹1,39,479 on net worth of ₹42,91,664), split into two unequal parts. Two-thirds, about ₹92,986 or 2.17% of NAV, is the core order at ₹2,070. It buys 44 whole shares for ₹91,080. One-third, about ₹46,493 or 1.08% of NAV, is the conditional order at ₹2,150. It buys 21 shares for ₹45,150. Place it only if, on the day, the base value re-run with the latest quarter's results and guidance is at least ₹2,367.50 on basis A. That needs FY27E EPS of about ₹131.5, which means FY27 EBITDA of roughly ₹331 Cr or more. Otherwise skip it and keep the cash. If it is skipped, the first fill is about 2.17% of NAV, not 3.25%. If both fill, the total is 65 shares for ₹1,36,230 (3.17% of NAV) at a blended ₹2,095.85. A daily close below ₹2,005 would then lose about ₹5,905, roughly 14 bps of NAV. At the bear value of ₹1,630 the loss would be about ₹30,280, or 0.71% of NAV. For scale, a full 6.5% position filled at ₹2,150 is 129 shares, and its loss at ₹1,630 would be ₹67,080. That is why the rest of the tier cap is not released without a fresh re-underwrite.

Decision ladder: what the tape must do, and what we would do about it
ScenarioWhat has to happenRecommendation
A. Pullback into supportPrice trades into ₹2,070 to ₹2,150, preferably on lighter volume with a daily close back above the 4H and 10-day averagesTwo-thirds (about 2.17% of NAV, 44 shares) at ₹2,070 is the core order. One-third (about 1.08%, 21 shares) at ₹2,150 is placed only if the re-derived base value is at least ₹2,367.50 on the year-end basis (about ₹331 Cr FY27 EBITDA). Stop: daily close below ₹2,005.
B. Stage 2 re-entry without a dipWeekly close above the 30-week average with the 10-week EMA turning up and volume expanding. Or a weekly close above the falling 50-day average (about ₹2,500) with the 30-week average rising.Not an automatic add, and it does not release the rest of the 6.5% cap. It re-opens the file for a re-underwrite. The base value is re-derived from the latest results, the stop is re-based, and any add must clear 1.5:1 from the add price to the new stop. The current bases (₹2,410 and ₹2,241) are both below ₹2,500, so at that price today's numbers give a negative reward.
C. Break of ₹2,005Daily close below ₹2,005 (the stop). A weekly close below the 30-week average (₹2,262.01) is the separate Stage 4 confirmation signal.Stage 4 confirmed: next shelves ₹1,810.97 (TradingView S2), then ₹1,336 to ₹1,393 (the Mar 2026 base). Cancel the alert and do not average down.
D. Drift sideways at ₹2,250 to ₹2,350Neither level is reached for weeks while volume stays near 0.25xNo position. The base-building that Weinstein requires takes time; the opportunity cost is low.

There are two ways back into the stock. One is a pullback into the support zone with the stop in place. The other is a confirmed new base that proves the decline is over, followed by a fresh re-underwrite. A daily close below ₹2,005 is the third state. In that case the same discipline says step aside, not average down [92], [91].

Scenario C deserves a plain description. If the daily close breaks ₹2,005, the loss is about 6.7% from ₹2,150 and 3.1% from ₹2,070. The technical picture also changes character. The 200-day, the last intact long-term average, would be lost, and the weekly 30-week line would be falling. The nearest shelf is TradingView's S2 at ₹1,810.97, which is 9.7% below the stop. Below that, the March 2026 base at ₹1,336 to ₹1,393 is where the stock turned the last time the market doubted the business. We do not forecast that. We simply refuse to hold a plan that depends on it not happening. Management's own words on capital allocation show why this matters to the stock and not just to the chart. The CEO told the Q2 FY27 call, “capital must earn its return within 12 months, or it goes back to the shareholders” [2]. The same call said distributions resume only after acquisition capital is deployed. Until then, shareholders have no yield to be paid for waiting.

What would change our technical view. Upgrade (Stage 2 re-entry or confirmed support): a weekly close above the 30-week average (₹2,262.01) with the 10-week EMA (₹2,416.11) turning up. Weekly volume should be at least 2x the recent 20,000-share-a-day norm in the breakout week. A weekly close above the falling 50-day average (about ₹2,500), with the 30-week average rising, would also qualify. Either event re-opens the file for a fresh re-underwrite. It does not release the rest of the 6.5% cap. The base value is re-derived from the latest results, the stop is re-based to the new base and 200-day, and any add must clear 1.5:1 from the add price to the new stop. At ₹2,500 the current base values (₹2,410 and ₹2,241) give a negative reward. Entry trigger: price inside ₹2,070 to ₹2,150, with volume drying up (below about 0.5x the 20-day average) and no daily close under ₹2,005. The ₹2,070 order is the core. The ₹2,150 order is conditional on a re-derived base value of at least ₹2,367.50 on the year-end basis. Downgrade (Stage 4 confirmed): a weekly close clearly below the 30-week average, or a daily close below ₹2,005. A second heavy-volume down session (above 40,000 shares) that closes under ₹2,150 without a reversal would also count. Alert flow: the Dec 2026 shareholding filing should show whether domestic institutions kept buying below ₹2,700. A rise above 2.5% would be the first sign of a real sponsor.

Everything in this chapter feeds one conclusion, which chapter 9 turns into the verdict. The business is a pass. The tape is a Stage 3 / Stage 4 boundary with distribution volume and a falling 50-day average. The sensible plan is to wait for the support zone or a confirmed base. Chapter 9 puts the numbers behind the sizing and the three-horizon call. It also explains why the reviewed capital stays in cash until one of the triggers above is met.

Chapter 9

The locked decision. Pass 1 closed on a business-quality verdict of COMPOUNDER_PASS with KPI health PASS_WITH_WATCH. Pass 2 now prices that business, and the answer is different. Our new-capital verdict is WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT, conviction TIER_2 (archetype QUALITY_COMPOUNDER_STAGE3_PULLBACK, strategy bucket CORE_ALPHA). We allocate zero at the current price. The portfolio holds no MPS shares (NOT_HELD, 0 shares), so the decision concerns new capital only. The reasoning is short. At the verified ledger close of ₹2,297.00 on 12 November 2026, the probability-weighted 12-month value is ₹2,440 on our central earnings basis, a gain of only 6.2%. On the stricter basis it is ₹2,254, a loss of 1.9%. The reward-to-risk against our ₹2,005 invalidation level is 0.39:1 on the central basis, far under our 1.5:1 hurdle. On the stricter basis it is negative. From ₹2,150, the top of the support zone, the central ratio is 1.79:1 and the stricter ratio is 0.63:1. From ₹2,070, the bottom, they are 5.23:1 and 2.63:1. The later 13 November print of ₹2,259.30 comes from the TradingView pull after the NSE close and is not yet ledger-verified. It improves the central ratio only to 0.59:1. Under the snapshot’s stricter classifier the stock has already slipped to Stage 4, because it sits 0.12% under its 30-week average. So the stock is at the Stage 3/4 boundary. The business passes, and the price and timing do not yet. The rest of this chapter puts the strongest bull and bear arguments side by side, shows the scenario arithmetic on both earnings bases and sets the evidence gates. It then sizes the position we would take if the price came to us.

Bull vs Bear: nine pillars, steelmanned on each side, with our independent judgment
PillarSteelman bullSteelman bear / skepticOur independent judgment
Franchise and moatA 56-year-old production and platform franchise embedded in the workflows of the largest scholarly and education publishers. Customers now buy three and four lines of work where they once bought one; the average count of MPS business units inside a star account rose from 2.5 to 5.5 over two years; ten-year operating cash flow roughly equals ten-year profit and ROCE has stayed in the mid-30s to high-30s since FY23 [2], [18], [1].Our own moat scorecard averages about 3 out of 5: relationships and Unbound retention score 4, but certifications, “AI-first” and labour cost score 2 to 3, and every competitor from Straive to codemantra sells the same AI pitch. Springer Nature built nonsense-text and image detectors in house and donated one to the STM Integrity Hub, so detection alone can be pulled in-house [29], [61], [58].Bull wins, about 70%: the franchise keeps earning above its cost of capital through 2030. It is a respectable moat built on relationships and platforms, not a wide technological one.
AI price deflationResearch EBITDA margin rose from 36.8% (FY25) to 39.9% (FY26) and then to 45.1% and 48.2% in Q1 and Q2 FY27 while Research employee cost fell 13.4% in FY26. The CEO’s framing is that customers share the productivity gain but MPS recovers it through volume, and management says 25% of revenue is now outcome-based [1], [25], [2].RWS shows what the early damage looks like: FY25 revenue fell 4% while adjusted pre-tax profit fell from £106.7m to £60.4m. MPS’s own FY20 saw book-production prices cut about 20% on average. The large mandates have not yet been renewed under AI-era pricing, the 25% outcome-based share has no published definition, and FY26 outsourcing cost grew 9.7% against revenue growth of 5.7% [67], [12], [1].Bear wins on direction, bull on level for the next four quarters. We put the weak form (continued deflation, MPS running to stand still) at about 80% and the strong form (margin below FY25’s 29.0% group level within three years) at about 20%; Research margin staying above 40% is about 70% and holding within three points of 48.2% only about 35%. This is the hinge of the whole case.
Customer concentrationThe largest customer fell from 18.6% to 17.3% of revenue and the top 15 from 61.1% to 58.0% (audited); top-five share fell to 46% from 51% in Q2 FY27, and clients billed roughly doubled to about 840 as Unbound’s institutional base arrived. Contracts typically run three to five years [1], [2].58% of revenue comes from 15 customers and, on the standalone entity, three customers above 10% each (39.0% of standalone revenue). AJE’s B2B referral channel was “entirely dependent on one publisher”. Appen lost a 30% customer and saw its largest division fall 38% within a year [1], [10], [37].Bear wins on level, bull on direction. Concentration improved over the last year. The largest customer fell from 18.6% to 17.3%, the top 15 from 61.1% to 58.0% and the top five from 51% to 46%. But the largest customer is still well above its FY23 level of 11.9%. One procurement decision can still move group margin. We put the chance of a top-three customer loss, a re-tender at lower rates, or a largest customer above 20% within 12 months at about 15%.
Corporate LearningH1 FY27 EBITDA margin about 25% against about 16%, EBITDA up 77% with 19% fewer people, Q2 revenue up 17%; Liberate is now wholly owned and a new segment president is in place. A turn here would add earnings on a small base (12.5% of FY26 revenue) [2], [1].FY26 revenue fell 16.5% and segment result fell from ₹20.00 Cr to ₹11.31 Cr. Management has missed three separate recovery dates; the corporate market grows 12% to 13% against MPS’s 6% to 7%; the segment head herself says “this is not a recovery of the legacy business”; and ₹100.38 Cr of goodwill sits against an ₹11.31 Cr segment result, 8.9 times [25], [19], [1].Skeptic wins on the thesis, bull on the margin. Treat it as an option on a turnaround, not a pillar. We put the margin staying above 18% for the next four quarters at about 70%, and growth back near 12% by FY28 at about 35%.
Acquisitions and goodwillEntry prices have been disciplined: Unbound at US$16.5 million was 2.09 times its annual revenue and 23.6 times its profit; AJE costing about ₹70 Cr “earns more than half of that back every year now”. The CEO: “I would rather miss a deal than overpay.” MPS is debt-free and says Unbound is “our new template” [1], [2].Goodwill of ₹376.32 Cr is 40% of total assets and goodwill plus acquired intangibles 52.5%. AJE’s provisional goodwill was cut 28% in the measurement period; Liberate has already been impaired by ₹12.93 Cr; the Unbound allocation is provisional until February 2027 and 88% of its price is goodwill. Management promised “five deals lined up” and did one in twelve months [1], [17].Bear wins on integration record, bull on price discipline. We put the chance of a goodwill impairment above ₹25 Cr in the next 12 months at about 10%, and treat a large new deal as a risk event, not a catalyst, until the first six months of its numbers are public.
Cash conversion and capital returnOperating cash flow over ten years is 1.00 times profit; capex is about 1.1% of revenue; FY26 free cash flow after leases was ₹23.89 per ₹100 of revenue; ₹651 Cr (about 99% of FY19 to FY25 profit) went back to shareholders; ₹163 Cr cash and no debt at 30 September 2026. The CEO: “the business is throwing out INR15 crores to INR18 crores of cash every month” [1], [25], [2].FY25 operating cash flow was only 68% of profit; FY26’s 83% of EBITDA came in a year when working capital was neutral; about ₹14 Cr of FY26 pre-tax profit was exceptional gain or liability write-back; no FY26 final dividend was declared and the CEO says distributions resume only “once that capital is deployed”, so the current yield is nil [1], [2].Bull wins, about 75% that free cash flow after leases stays above 80% of profit. But the payout record is a past fact, not a current cash yield, and a new acquisition delays it again.
Governance and the ADI BPO mergerThe promoter holds 68.34% and has not sold; the amalgamation puts ADI BPO’s own shareholders directly on the MPS register; MPS says public shareholders are not diluted; the equity meeting approved it with all promoter and institutional votes and 1,52,327 of 1,52,377 non-institutional votes in favour; the CEO and CFO are the continuity [1], [11], [95].One person is chairman and CEO and was paid 191 times the median employee in FY26, up 31.33%; the company secretary, the CFO and the Corporate Learning heads have changed; the audit-trail exception has repeated for three years; the court-convened vote was carried mainly by the promoter’s own 68%; and final sanction is unconfirmed [1], [96].Slight bear until final sanction, about 80% that the scheme closes as described. The residual risks are overhang if new direct holders sell, and any change in terms; the latter is a cancel trigger.
ValuationEnterprise value of ₹3,784 Cr is 13.6 times trailing EBITDA of ₹279 Cr and 12.0 times our FY27E EBITDA of ₹315 Cr. P/E on FY27E EPS of about ₹124.5 is 18.4 times, and the FCFF yield is 5.7%. The reverse DCF needs average FCF growth of about 8.8% a year along the fade path (12.6% in year one fading to 5%), at a 13% discount rate. EBITDA compounded 17.1% a year over FY21 to FY26. MPS has the highest ROCE and margin in its peer set [2], [1].22.7x trailing earnings equals the five-year median and is near the 75th percentile of the ten-year band, so there is no discount to history. Year-one growth of 12.6% is implied at a 13% discount rate, above the ten-year EBITDA CAGR of 10.2%. Dividend yield is nil. On P/E, MPS (20.74) trades about 10% above Datamatics (18.87) and 6% above Newgen (19.6). It is well above NIIT Learning Systems (9.87). Its ROCE of 38.7% against peers’ 20.4% to 25.0% explains a premium, but the premium is already in the price.A draw: fair, not cheap. It is a fair price for a high-quality business, not a margin of safety. At the 18x base multiple, central-basis EPS gives only +4.9% from ₹2,297.00. The stricter year-end basis gives -2.4%. At ₹2,150 the stock is on 17.3 times FY27E EPS and 11.2 times FY27E EBITDA, closer to the ten-year median.
TapeThe 200-day average is rising (₹2,069.06, up from ₹1,918.86 sixty-three sessions earlier); the stock gained 38% in six months; it has compounded 26.1% a year over five years; and all three listed peers (Datamatics, NIIT Learning Systems, Newgen) are in Stage 4 and down 18% to 56% over a year while MPS is roughly flat.Close below a falling 50-day average (₹2,517 on the ledger, down from ₹2,704), RSI 42, nine distribution days in 25 sessions, volume at 0.36 times its 20-day average, relative strength of -26.7% over 28 days, a close 22.9% under the 52-week high, and a snapshot classification of Stage 4 with the timing gate rejected. Three drawdowns of 50% or more since 2015 (-57.7%, -67.5%, -51.9%).Bear wins for the next one to three months, about 60/40. The 30-week average at ₹2,262 is the shelf: the 13 November close of ₹2,259.30 is sitting on it, and a weekly close below it would confirm Stage 4. We act on the rising 200-day average and the swing low, not on the shelf.

The bull case wins the debate on business quality: franchise and cash conversion. Concentration is improving but still elevated. The bear case wins on the three things that decide the price over the next year. Those are AI price give-back at renewal, the quality of acquired assets and the tape. Valuation is a draw. At 22.7x trailing earnings the stock sits on the five-year median and near the 75th percentile (23.0x) of the ten-year band. That band has a median of 17.6x, a 25th percentile of 13.8x and a maximum of 41.7x. So the multiple is reasonable, not a bargain. Probabilities in the last column are desk estimates, not company guidance. They are consistent with the odds stated in Chapters 4 and 5.

On the central basis the probability-weighted value is ₹2,440, a gain of 6.2% from ₹2,297.00. The distribution is skewed the way a quality compounder’s should be: bull +44.1% against bear -29.1%. But half the weight sits on the base case, which pays only 4.9%. The multiples map to history. The 14x bear multiple is close to the ten-year 25th percentile (13.8x). The 18x base multiple matches the ten-year median (17.6x), and the 22x bull multiple matches the five-year median (22.7x). All scenarios assume D&A of ₹36 Cr in FY27E and ₹38 Cr in FY28E, other income of ₹8 Cr, interest of ₹3 Cr, tax of 25% and 1.7106 Cr shares. The 25/50/25 weights are a judgment. Moving to 35/45/20 would lower this weighted value to about ₹2,317. The verdict does not depend on the weights, because the decision rule is the reward-to-risk at the support zone.

The same scenarios on two earnings bases (value date about November 2027; multiples 14x / 18x / 22x)

The ten-year P/E band is built on last-reported fiscal-year EPS. At a November 2027 value date, the matching EPS on the band’s own basis is FY27E. That is basis A, the stress case. Basis B uses last-twelve-months EPS at the value date, the average of FY27E and FY28E. It is our central case, because markets price on current earnings and a lagging EPS understates what the market typically pays. Basis B is the more generous reading, and we do not call the two the same basis. On basis A the weighted value is slightly below today’s price. That is why the position is sized so that the verdict survives the stricter reading.

The EPS and multiple logic. Each scenario multiplies two numbers: what the business earns and what the market pays for a rupee of it. The multiples come from the ten-year band of P/E on last-reported fiscal-year EPS. We show two earnings bases. Basis A uses FY27E EPS of ₹117.9, ₹124.5 and ₹131.1. Basis B uses last-twelve-months EPS at the value date, taken as the average of FY27E and FY28E: ₹116.4, ₹133.9 and ₹150.4. FY26 EPS was ₹101.26 on the aggregator’s basis (₹102.11 audited basic). Our base-case LTM EPS of ₹133.9 is 32.2% above that. The driver is the guided FY27 EBITDA floor of at least ₹310 Cr, raised from ₹300 Cr on 4 November 2026. H1 FY27 EBITDA was already ₹154 Cr. On top of that floor we assume about 14% growth in FY28 [2]. That growth rate is below the 17.1% EBITDA CAGR of FY21 to FY26 and above the 10.2% ten-year CAGR, a deliberate middle. The base multiple of 18x is lower than today’s 22.7x trailing. We ask the market to pay less per rupee of earnings, and the result is a base return of only 4.9% on basis B and -2.4% on basis A. The bear value of ₹1,630 is 24.2% below the ₹2,150 entry zone. That matters for sizing, because our ₹2,005 daily-close stop is a risk-control level, not a worst case.

At the ₹2,297.00 ledger close the stock offers ₹113 of upside to the central base case against ₹292 of risk to the stop. That is 0.39:1, far below our 1.5:1 hurdle. On basis A the base value is below the price, so the ratio is negative. The 13 November print of ₹2,259.30 gives 0.59:1 on basis B and -0.07:1 on basis A. The ₹2,070 tranche clears 1.5:1 on both bases, at 5.23:1 and 2.63:1. It is the core order. The ₹2,150 tranche clears 1.5:1 only on basis B, at 1.79:1. On basis A it is 0.63:1. The ₹2,150 tranche needs a base value of at least ₹2,367.50. Basis B clears that by 1.8%, and basis A misses it by ₹126.5. So the ₹2,150 tranche is conditional. An expected return of +6.2% on the central basis is about half of what a 13% cost of equity demands. You are not paid for the risk.

Why a limit buy is consistent with a Stage 4 reading. We take the stricter Stage 4 reading to forbid buying at the current price or on a bounce. The limit buys at ₹2,070 and ₹2,150 are a different, deliberate trade. They are a half-size, counter-trend support entry at the rising 200-day average (₹2,069), inside a long-term uptrend. That average still slopes up and was ₹1,919 sixty-three sessions ago. The ₹2,070 order sits right on that average and clears our reward-to-risk hurdle on both earnings bases (2.63:1 and 5.23:1). It is the order most consistent with the Stage 4 reading. The ₹2,150 order clears the hurdle only on the central basis (1.79:1), so it is conditional. We abandon both orders if the daily close breaks ₹2,005. This is not a Stage 2 entry, and it does not claim that the trend has turned.

Independent verdict across three horizons.
1. Short term (1 to 3 months): WAIT. The tape is at the Stage 3/4 boundary. The close of ₹2,297 on 12 November (₹2,259.30 on 13 November) is under a falling 50-day average of about ₹2,500 to ₹2,517. There are nine distribution days in 25 sessions and RSI is between 40 and 42. A weekly close under the 30-week shelf of ₹2,262 would confirm Stage 4. We see no edge in front-running either direction. The next evidence is the Q3 FY27 print and the Unbound allocation, due by February 2027 [1].
2. Medium term (6 to 18 months): ACCUMULATE only inside ₹2,070 to ₹2,150 with the ₹2,005 daily-close stop, or after a confirmed re-base and a fresh re-underwrite, provided the evidence gates hold. The core order is two-thirds of the starter at ₹2,070. The one-third at ₹2,150 is conditional: we place it only if the re-derived base value is at or above ₹2,367.50 on basis A. H2 FY27 EBITDA must be at least ₹156 Cr, so that FY27 reaches the ₹310 Cr floor after H1’s ₹154 Cr. Our base case needs about ₹161 Cr. Research EBITDA margin must stay at or above 45% and Corporate Learning margin above 18%. There must be no goodwill impairment above ₹25 Cr. The largest customer should stay at or below about 17% and the top-five share at or below 46%. A re-base means a weekly close above the 30-week average with the 10-week EMA (₹2,416 on the 13 November print) turning up on rising volume. A weekly close above the falling 50-day average near ₹2,500 with the 30-week average rising also re-opens the file. Neither is an automatic add.
3. Long term (3 to 5 years): a genuine compounder candidate (COMPOUNDER_PASS). The business-quality case is intact. Ten-year operating cash flow equals profit, capex is about 1% of revenue and the balance sheet is debt-free. The debate for this horizon is price, AI price give-back and acquisition discipline, not whether the franchise exists. Three prior 50%-plus drawdowns tell us the entry price decides whether a five-year holding feels like a compounder or a roller coaster.

Upgrade triggers are about confirmation, and none of them is an automatic add. Each one re-opens the file for a fresh re-underwrite. Any add must clear 1.5:1 from the add price to a re-based stop. One business trigger also activates the conditional ₹2,150 tranche. Cancel triggers are business-level and apply regardless of price. Any one of them removes MPS from the watch even if the stock reaches the support zone. They would break the Pass 1 case rather than reprice it. The ₹2,005 daily close is the only price-based cancel.

What we might be wrong about. The hinge claim of this chapter is that Research margin durability survives the AI price give-back at the next large renewal. Research is 60% of revenue and about two-thirds of segment profit. Its margin has risen from 36.8% in FY25 to 48.2% in Q2 FY27. The CEO’s own account is that customers “expect to share in our productivity” [1], [2]. The base case assumes the sharing is real but affordable, so price falls and volume rises by more. It needs Research margin in the mid-40s through the next AI-era re-bid. Suppose instead the first large re-bid resets unit rates by 15% to 20% with no matching volume commitment. That is the size of the FY20 offshore book-production cut. Research margin would then drop toward 40% within two quarters. The bear case’s ₹295 Cr FY28E EBITDA would be a reasonable outcome, and a purchase at ₹2,150 would have been made for the wrong reason. The margin does not need to fall that far to hurt. Two quarters under about 41% to 42% would hurt. So would a lower-rate re-tender of the 17.3% customer. A 20% re-tender of that customer is about ₹26.6 Cr of revenue. Flowing fully to EBITDA for a year, it takes the base value to about ₹2,306. For half a year it takes it to about ₹2,358. Both figures are on basis B, and both are below the ₹2,367.50 line for the ₹2,150 tranche. The error in the other direction is also real. The stock has already pulled back 22.9% from its high. If it reclaims ₹2,500 on volume without visiting ₹2,150, we forgo roughly the first 10% of that move. We accept that. At ₹2,500 our current base value of ₹2,410 would give a negative reward. A missed 10% costs no capital, while a bear-case ₹1,630 costs 24.2% from the top of the zone. Two further things could make this analysis wrong. Our P/E mapping assumes historical multiples are a sane guide for a business moving toward subscriptions and outcome-based pricing. If the market starts to treat MPS as a software-like compounder, 22x will look cheap and 14x will look absurd. And the half-size rule assumes thin liquidity and gap risk matter for a ₹1.4 lakh position. They do for exits but not for entries.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: Passing the business is not the same as buying the stock

Five chapters of the audit said yes. Cash matches profit, capex is near 1% of revenue, the balance sheet has no debt, and margins rose through an AI shift. It is tempting to let that momentum carry the decision. The discipline of this chapter is to separate two questions that a rising price blurs together. Is this a good business, and is this a good price for it? The first answer is yes, with real conditions. The second is “not yet, and here is the price at which it becomes yes”.

A 6.2% expected return at 0.39:1 is not an ordinary bet. It is a poor one. On the stricter earnings basis the expected return is negative. Such a bet in a stock that fell 51.9% and then rose 110.5% inside two years does not deserve an allocation. Waiting for ₹2,070, and only conditionally ₹2,150, is not pessimism about MPS. It is respect for how violently this particular stock has been priced.

No-FOMO support entry zone, stop placement & position sizing

Position sizing decides how much of your capital rides on one idea, so that a wrong call costs a planned amount and not a surprise. Tier TIER_2 carries a range of 4% to 6.5% of net worth and a risk budget of 75 bps of net worth. On net worth of ₹42,91,664 that budget is ₹32,187. At an entry of ₹2,150 and a stop of ₹2,005, the stop sits 6.74% below entry (₹145 a share). The raw risk-unit weight is 0.75% divided by 6.74%, or 11.1%. The tier cap limits it to 6.5% (₹2,78,958). Our rule for MPS is a starter of about half the cap, 3.25% of net worth (₹1,39,479), in two tranches. The core order is two-thirds of the starter, about 2.17% (₹92,986), at ₹2,070. It clears 1.5:1 on both earnings bases. The conditional order is one-third, about 1.08% (₹46,493), at ₹2,150. It clears 1.5:1 only on the central basis. We place it only if, on the day, the re-derived base value is at or above ₹2,367.50 on basis A. That needs FY27E EPS of about ₹131.5, or FY27 EBITDA of roughly ₹331 Cr or more. Otherwise we skip it and keep the cash. If it is skipped, the first fill is about 2.17% of net worth, not 3.25%. Three facts drive the half-size rule. The stock has had three drawdowns of 50% or more since 2015 (-57.7%, -67.5% and -51.9% on weekly closes). Liquidity is thin at roughly 15,000 to 19,000 shares a day. And a 6.74% stop is gap-prone in a stock like this. We release the rest of the cap only after a confirmed re-base and a fresh re-underwrite that clears 1.5:1. The compiler’s execution table that follows shows 0.0% and 0 bps today. That is because the verdict is WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT, so there is no live allocation until the price is in the zone. The 75 bps risk budget and the 6.5% cap apply once a tranche is triggered.

This execution table translates our WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT verdict on MPSLTD into concrete price levels, invalidation stops, and portfolio risk-budget sizing.

Rupee examples on net worth of ₹42,91,664. In whole shares, the core order at ₹2,070 buys 44 shares for ₹91,080 (2.12% of net worth). The conditional order at ₹2,150 buys 21 shares for ₹45,150 (1.05%). If both fill, that is 65 shares costing ₹1,36,230 (3.17% of net worth) at a blended ₹2,095.85. If only the core order fills, the first fill is ₹91,080. If the stock closes through ₹2,005 and we exit there, the loss is ₹5,905 with both orders and ₹2,860 with the core order alone. That is 0.14% and 0.07% of net worth, well inside the 75 bps budget. We also test the sizing against the bear case, not just the stop. If the stock fell to the bear value of ₹1,630, the full starter would lose about ₹30,280 (0.71% of net worth), just inside the budget. The core order alone would lose about ₹19,360 (0.45%). A full 6.5% position filled at ₹2,150 is 129 shares (₹2,77,350). It would lose about ₹67,080 (1.56% of net worth) at ₹1,630, more than twice the budget. At the full cap the loss to the stop alone is ₹18,705 (0.44%), which is inside the budget. It is gap risk, not the stop, that argues for half size. Liquidity. Median daily volume is about 14,680 shares over the last 20 sessions and about 17,615 over the last 250. Median daily traded value is about ₹3.6 Cr. Our 65 shares are about 0.4% of one median day’s volume and of one day’s value, so entry will not move the price. The exit is the problem. Volume was 6,719 shares on 12 November (0.36 times its 20-day average) and 7,306 on 13 November. A stop at ₹2,005 is a trigger, not a guarantee of that price. We treat it as an alert and review it daily instead of relying on an automatic order. Cash. Cash is ₹5,74,582 (13.4% of net worth). The full starter leaves about ₹4,38,352 (10.2%). The core order alone leaves about ₹4,83,502 (11.3%). Cash covers the full starter about 4.2 times and even the full 6.5% cap about 2.1 times.

Corporate-structure watch item: the ADI BPO amalgamation. ADI BPO Services Ltd, the promoter holding company, owns 1,16,90,615 of MPS’s 1,71,05,816 shares (68.34%). The board approved the amalgamation of ADI BPO into MPS on 18 July 2025. The equity shareholders approved it on 22 August 2026. The NCLT Chennai second-motion order is dated 16 September 2026, and a further hearing was listed for 4 November 2026 [97], [96]. We could not find the outcome of that hearing, so final sanction is unconfirmed. A trade-press headline reading “NCLT approves” refers to the 16 September order. That order directed notices to the Ministry of Corporate Affairs, the Registrar of Companies, tax authorities and the Official Liquidator, with 30 days for representations. It is a procedural step, not the final sanction [96], [98]. MPS describes the mechanics this way. ADI BPO first demerges its infrastructure-management business into ADI Holdings Pvt Ltd. It then merges into MPS and dissolves without winding up. ADI BPO’s 68.34% holding in MPS is cancelled, and the same number of MPS shares is issued to ADI BPO’s own shareholders on a share exchange ratio. There is no cash consideration, and the scheme is designed to be tax-neutral [95], [98]. If those statements hold, the public stays at 54,15,201 shares, 31.66% of the register.

Nothing in the record we read suggests a loss for public shareholders. The near-unanimous non-institutional vote (1,52,327 for, 50 against) is consistent with that. But four checks remain open. The one with a price consequence is the overhang. 1% of MPS is 1,71,058 shares, about 11.7 days of median volume or about ₹39 Cr at ₹2,297. A change in terms is a cancel trigger.

How to read the panels that follow. The holding-action, alerts and redeployment panels rendered after this chapter apply the decision above. The holding action is NOT_HELD with 0 shares, so there is nothing to sell. The limit-zone alerts are ₹2,070, the core order for two-thirds of the starter, and ₹2,150, a conditional order for one-third. We place the ₹2,150 order only if the re-derived base value is at or above ₹2,367.50 on basis A. The cancel alert fires on a daily close below ₹2,005. The upgrade alert is a weekly close above about ₹2,500. It means re-underwrite, not add. The redeployment panel earmarks 100% of the reviewed capital as cash for MPS support-zone entries. It gives 0% to AJMERA, an existing 4.06%-weight holding with its own dossier whose sizing is a portfolio-audit decision, not funded here. It gives 0% to outside peers, because Datamatics, NIIT Learning Systems and Newgen are all Stage 4 and rejected by the timing gate. The panels are recommendations and alerts for you to act on, not standing orders. MPS is not in the NSE futures-and-options segment, so cash equity is the only instrument.

NARRATOR'S REFLECTION: Cash is a position

When the plan says “wait”, it is tempting to read it as inaction. It is not. A 13.4% cash balance with a named place to go is an active position. ₹5,74,582 is earmarked for a stock we like, at a price we have computed, with a cancel level and a list of things that would change our mind. The alternative is buying a half-position now because the business is good and the stock is down 22.9%. The numbers have not asked for that position.

The discipline also protects against the opposite mistake. Suppose the stock never returns to ₹2,070 and instead reclaims ₹2,500 on volume. The upgrade trigger then re-opens the file. It does not tell us to buy. Missing a 10% rally is cheap. Owning a bear-case ₹1,630 position at full size is not.

Re-underwrite triggers (upgrade): move from WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT to a first fill only when the stock trades inside ₹2,070 to ₹2,150, the ₹2,005 stop is intact on a daily close and no business-level cancel trigger is active. The core order is two-thirds of the starter at ₹2,070. The one-third order at ₹2,150 is conditional. We place it only if the re-derived base value, re-run with the latest quarter’s results and guidance, is at or above ₹2,367.50 on basis A. That needs FY27 EBITDA of roughly ₹331 Cr or more. Otherwise we skip it and keep the cash. A weekly close above the falling 50-day average near ₹2,500 with the 30-week average rising is not an automatic add. It does not release the rest of the 6.5% cap. It re-opens the file for a fresh re-underwrite. We re-derive the base value from the latest results, because the current ₹2,410 central base is below ₹2,500. We re-base the stop to the new base and the 200-day average. Any add must clear 1.5:1 from the add price to the new stop. The same test applies after a weekly close above the 30-week average with the 10-week EMA turning up. We also want H2 FY27 EBITDA tracking at least ₹156 Cr (FY27 at or above the ₹310 Cr floor), Research margin at or above 45%, Corporate Learning margin above 18% and the ADI BPO scheme sanctioned without dilution. Cancel the watch on any of these. A daily close below ₹2,005. Research margin below 42% for two quarters. A largest customer above 20%, or a top-three customer loss. Corporate Learning margin back below 18% for two quarters. Any goodwill impairment above ₹25 Cr. An FY27 EBITDA guidance cut below ₹300 Cr. ADI BPO terms changing to be dilutive to public holders.

The tape sits at the Stage 3/Stage 4 boundary. Only the ₹2,070 end of the support zone clears 1.5:1 on both earnings bases (5.23:1 and 2.63:1).

Run notes and gate exceptions

Session date. The 13 November 2026 Nifty 50 bar was absent from the price source after two attempts (ledger build and a later retry), so the report is dated to the last verified session, 12 November 2026. MPSLTD closed at ₹2,297.00 in the ledger that day.

The TradingView and stage snapshot was pulled after the 13 November NSE close and carries that day's print of ₹2,259.30 (-1.64%). It is not benchmark-verified, so prices and verdict levels use the ledger close and the 13 November print is cited as later. The ledger labels the stock Stage 3, while the snapshot classifier labels it Stage 4 because ₹2,259.30 is 0.12% under the 30-week average of ₹2,262.01. The stricter reading governs timing.

TheWrap Stage 2 dashboard was extracted in Chrome and MPSLTD is not on the Stage 2 list. The options tool's auto-text called the setup a call-spread window above the weekly shelf. The 13 November print is under the shelf and MPSLTD has no NSE options chain, so the desk replaced that sentence in a copy of the options snapshot. The raw tool output is unchanged.

Holdings records were not refreshed for this session. MPSLTD is not held, so no position action depends on them.

Source gaps and disclosed discrepancies: the Q4 FY26 earnings-call transcript was read from a machine-generated source and an exchange filing; no Q2 FY27 segment tables were read, so Q2 FY27 segment figures come from the earnings call and its slides; Screener and audited figures differ on debtor days, ROCE, EPS and other income and each chapter states the audited basis; the NCLT hearing of 4 November 2026 on the ADI BPO amalgamation had no reported outcome at the time of writing.

Citation as-of exceptions (older than 60 days, latest available filings): MPSLTD:fy26_consolidated_results and MPSLTD:fy26_eps_net_cash (FY26 audited annual report, 2026-03-31; cure with the FY27 annual report in mid-2027), MPSLTD:promoter_holding (31 March 2026 holding in the annual report; the September 2026 shareholding table shows the same 68.34%) and MPSLTD:outcome_based_recurring_mix (Q1 FY27 call, August 2026; cure with the Q3 FY27 call in February 2027).

Review status: BEST_EFFORT_AFTER_REVIEW_LIMIT. Two review rounds ran. The red team's second pass held the verdict. The independent reviewer's second pass asked for two changes: show the scenario values on both earnings bases, and remove process wording. Both were made after round 2 and were not re-reviewed, because the review cap is two rounds. The reviewer and red team ran on the default model rather than the configured red-team model, which was not available under that identifier.
